Full answer
-----------

VA’s 2024 rule expands eligibility for veterans with entry-level separations and separations for the convenience of the government—the categories DoD’s Discharge Appeal Review Board treats as less than honorable. Holders of those separations can seek that board’s review if they were discharged on or after October 1, 1982.

Full answer
-----------

Based on the documents:

Separations DARB treats as “less than honorable” The DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) defines a “characterization of less than honorable” to include:

- general (under honorable conditions)
- other than honorable conditions
- uncharacterized
- bad-conduct discharge
- dishonorable discharge
- dismissal

(32 CFR 73.2).

Where VA’s 2024 rule expands eligibility among them VA’s January 2024 final rule (38 CFR 3.12, effective June 25, 2024) modifies the “regulatory bars” to benefits for these characterizations. It expands eligibility mainly for holders of other-than-honorable, bad-conduct, and comparable dismissals/discharges that were previously barred because of:

- willful and persistent misconduct or moral turpitude — now these bars can be overcome if “compelling circumstances” exist (new paragraph (e)); and
- homosexual acts — the prior regulatory bar is removed entirely.

Bars for discharge in lieu of general court-martial and for mutiny/espionage remain without a compelling-circumstances exception, so eligibility is not expanded for those.

Discharge date for DARB review A holder of one of those less-than-honorable separations may seek the DARB’s final review only if the discharge or dismissal occurred on or after December 20, 2019 (32 CFR 73.5(a)(1)). They must also have exhausted all remedies at their service’s DRB and BCM/NR and received a denial or only partial grant of an upgrade.

Let's read Document 3 carefully.

In Document 3, they revise paragraph (d) "Regulatory bars to benefits." The bars are:
(1) Compelling circumstances exception is not applicable for:
(i) Discharge in lieu of trial. Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial.
(ii) Mutiny or espionage. Mutiny or spying.

(2) Compelling circumstances exception is applicable for:
(i) An offense involving moral turpitude. This paragraph (d)(2)(i) includes, generally, conviction of a felony.
(ii) Willful and persistent misconduct. ...

So they kept four regulatory bars? Wait they said in summary: "there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct."

Previously (before 2024 rule) there were five regulatory bars listed in old 38 CFR 3.12(d): (1) acceptance of an undesirable discharge to avoid general court-martial; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) an offense involving moral turpitude; (4) homosexual acts involving aggravating circumstances; (5) willful and persistent misconduct? - But also they kept bar for discharge in lieu of GCM and mutiny/spying without compelling circumstances.

For which of those less-than-honorable categories does the VA rule now allow benefits (where previously barred)? Let's think.

Previously, VA's regulatory bars (old 3.12(d)) listed specific conditions that are considered dishonorable:
(a) discharge in lieu of GCM (undesirable)
(b) mutiny/spying
(c) moral turpitude
(d) homosexual acts involving aggravating circumstances
(e) willful and persistent misconduct

Also statutory bars in 3.12(c) include conscientious objector, deserter (AWOL 180+ days), resignation for good of service, discharge by GCM, etc.

The 2024 rule removes (d) homosexual acts entirely. So for individuals with OTH discharges due to homosexual acts, VA now expands eligibility (no longer barred). Also for willful and persistent misconduct and moral turpitude, the rule now allows a compelling circumstances exception, which can overcome the bar. So eligibility is expanded for those with OTH/BCD discharges based on those reasons if compelling circumstances exist.

Based on the documents:

Separations affected DoD’s Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) counts as “less than honorable” a discharge/dismissal that is: general (under honorable conditions), other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, dishonorable, or a dismissal (32 CFR 73.2, “Characterization of less than honorable”).

VA’s 2024 character-of-discharge rule (38 CFR Part 3, final rule effective June 25, 2024) expands benefit eligibility for holders of these separations by:

- Removing the prior regulatory bar for “homosexual acts involving aggravating circumstances”;
- Refining the “willful and persistent misconduct” bar with objective time-frame criteria; and
- Applying a “compelling circumstances” exception to both the “moral turpitude” and “willful and persistent misconduct” bars (and to prolonged AWOL), allowing benefits when mental/cognitive impairment, trauma, abuse, hardship, age/education, or other mitigating factors are present.

So the expansion applies to the OTH, bad-conduct, and other non-honorable separations (including dismissals) that previously fell under the now-removed or modified bars—provided the other statutory bars (e.g., discharge in lieu of general court-martial, mutiny/spying) do not apply.

Discharge date for DARB review Under 32 CFR 73.5(a)(1), a petitioner may seek the DARB’s final review only if the discharge or dismissal occurred on or after December 20, 2019 (and the petitioner has exhausted the Military Department’s DRB and BCM/NR remedies and was denied or only partially granted relief).

Full answer
-----------

Based on the documents:

Separations DARB treats as “less than honorable” The DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) defines “characterization of less than honorable” to include:

- general (under honorable conditions)
- other than honorable conditions
- uncharacterized
- bad‑conduct discharge
- dishonorable discharge
- dismissal

(32 CFR §73.2, “Characterization of less than honorable”)

Which of those does VA’s 2024 COD rule expand eligibility for? VA’s 2024 final rule (38 CFR Part 3, §3.12) keeps four regulatory bars but now allows a “compelling circumstances” exception for two of them and removes a fifth bar entirely. So benefit eligibility is expanded for holders of other‑than‑honorable, bad‑conduct, and dismissal separations (and similar less‑than‑honorable characterizations) that were based on:

1. Willful and persistent misconduct – now can be overcome if compelling circumstances mitigate it (§3.12(d)(2)(ii), (e)).
2. An offense involving moral turpitude – now also subject to the compelling‑circumstances exception (§3.12(d)(2)(i), (e)).
3. Homosexual acts – the prior regulatory bar for this was eliminated entirely, so those previously barred are now eligible (§3.12(d) removal of former homosexual‑acts bar).

The rule does not expand eligibility for separations based on accepting discharge in lieu of general court‑martial or for mutiny/espionage, because no compelling‑circumstances exception applies to those (§3.12(d)(1)).

Discharge date required to seek DARB review A holder of such a separation may petition the DARB only if the discharge or dismissal occurred on or after December 20, 2019 (§73.5(a)(1)). They must also have exhausted remedies at their service DRB and BCM/NR and received a denial or only partial grant (§73.5(a)(3)–(4)).

(d)(1) Compelling circumstances exception is not applicable for: (i) Discharge in lieu of trial... (ii) Mutiny or espionage.

(d)(2) Compelling circumstances exception is applicable for: (i) offense involving moral turpitude... (ii) willful and persistent misconduct...

Full answer
-----------

The DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) treats the following as “less than honorable” separations: a general (under honorable conditions) discharge, an other than honorable (OTH) discharge, an uncharacterized separation, a bad-conduct discharge, a dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal (32 CFR 73.2).

VA’s 2024 character-of-discharge rule (38 CFR 3.12, effective June 25, 2024) expands benefit eligibility for holders of those separations—especially OTH, bad-conduct, dismissal, general and uncharacterized discharges—that were previously barred because of:

- willful and persistent misconduct (now more narrowly defined and subject to a compelling-circumstances exception);
- an offense involving moral turpitude (now also subject to a compelling-circumstances exception);
- homosexual acts (the regulatory bar was removed entirely); or
- prolonged AWOL of 180 days or more (now subject to a compelling-circumstances exception).

So the rule chiefly benefits people whose non-honorable separation was based on one of those now-modified or removed regulatory grounds.

Under the DARB procedures, the holders of those separations may seek the board’s final review only if they were discharged or dismissed on or after December 20, 2019 (32 CFR 73.5(a)(1)), provided they received a less-than-honorable characterization and have exhausted their Military Department DRB and BCM/NR remedies.

Full answer
-----------

Separations affected

DoD’s Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) treats the following as “less than honorable”: a general (under honorable conditions) discharge, other than honorable (OTH) conditions, uncharacterized, bad‑conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal (32 CFR § 73.2).

VA’s 2024 character‑of‑discharge final rule (38 CFR Part 3, effective for applications received on or after June 25, 2024) expands benefit eligibility for holders of those separations—especially OTH, bad‑conduct, dishonorable, and dismissal—by:

- Removing entirely the old regulatory bar for discharges based on homosexual acts; and
- Adding a “compelling circumstances” exception to the remaining misconduct‑based bars for moral turpitude, willful and persistent misconduct, and prolonged AWOL (180 days or more) (38 CFR § 3.12(d)(2) and (e)).

The rule does not expand eligibility for the two unconditional bars that remain: acceptance of a discharge in lieu of trial by general court‑martial and discharges for mutiny or spying (38 CFR § 3.12(d)(1)).

Discharge date for DARB review

A service member (or authorized representative) may seek the DARB’s final review only if the discharge or dismissal occurred on or after December 20, 2019 (32 CFR § 73.5(a)(1)). The petitioner must also have exhausted all remedies at the service’s Discharge Review Board and Board for Correction of Military/Naval Records, and the BCM/NR must have denied (or only partially granted) the upgrade request.

Reasoning
---------

So DARB counts these as less than honorable.

Full answer
-----------

Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) and Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges; December 20, 2019

Reasoning
---------

I need to carefully analyze the question and find the relevant information in the provided documents.

The question asks:
"For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility, and from what discharge date can their holders seek that board's review?"

Let me break this down into two parts:
1. Which separations (discharge types) that DoD's DARB counts as "less than honorable" does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility for?
2. From what discharge date can holders of these separations seek DARB review?

First, I need to find information about DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) and what it considers "less than honorable" discharges.

From Document 5 (Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 230, November 29, 2024), which discusses the DARB:

On page 94605, footnote 42 states: "A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

So DARB considers these as "less than honorable":
- Dismissals
- General (under honorable conditions)
- Other than honorable (OTH)
- Uncharacterized
- Bad-conduct
- Dishonorable

Now, I need to find VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule and see which of these it expands benefit eligibility for.

Document 3 (Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 82, April 26, 2024) appears to be VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule. Let me examine this.

From Document 3, page 32362: "Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct."

And on page 32363: "It is important to clarify here that the regulatory bars shall only be applied when they are clearly supported by the military record. The benefit of the doubt will be resolved in favor of the former SM."

Also: "Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d). That said, a favorable COD determination under this rule does not result in blanket eligibility for all VA benefits or a change in the Department of Defense's (DoD) discharge characterization. Rather, certain VA benefits have specific eligibility requirements as it pertains to COD. For example, education assistance under the Montgomery GI Bill program or Post-9/11 GI Bill program is available only for periods of service resulting in an ``honorable'' discharge."

This suggests the VA rule expands eligibility for some with less than honorable discharges, but not all VA benefits.

Let me look for more specific information about which discharge types get expanded eligibility.

On page 32362: "Given those factors, with this rule, VA seeks to strike a balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them, even those whose service was not without blemish, and limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct."

On page 32364: "Between October 1, 2019, and September 30, 2022, VA deemed SMs with OTH discharges eligible for healthcare or benefits or both more than 75% of the time."

This suggests OTH (Other Than Honorable) discharges are a key focus.

Also, on page 32363: "Moreover, while relaxing the bars to eligibility, this final rule does not extend VA benefits eligibility to all former SMs. Former SMs who do not meet the criteria for benefits eligibility may remain entitled to certain critical benefits to address the harms caused by their military service such as mental health and substance use care, emergent suicide care, and medical care in emergency situations, as discussed below."

Let me check the regulatory bars section more carefully.

On page 32372-32373, the actual regulatory text shows:

"(d) Regulatory bars to benefits. Benefits are not payable where the former service member was discharged or released under one of the conditions listed in paragraph (d)(1) or (2) of this section.
(1) Compelling circumstances exception is not applicable for:
(i) Discharge in lieu of trial. Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial.
(ii) Mutiny or espionage. Mutiny or spying.
(2) Compelling circumstances exception is applicable for:
(i) An offense involving moral turpitude. This paragraph (d)(2)(i) includes, generally, conviction of a felony.
(ii) Willful and persistent misconduct."

So the regulatory bars (which would deny benefits) are:
1. Discharge in lieu of trial (OTH or equivalent in lieu of GCM) - NO compelling circumstances exception
2. Mutiny or spying - NO compelling circumstances exception
3. Moral turpitude - compelling circumstances exception APPLIES
4. Willful and persistent misconduct - compelling circumstances exception APPLIES

Previously, there were more regulatory bars, including one for homosexual acts, which has been removed.

The key change is that for moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct, there's now a "compelling circumstances exception" that can allow benefits despite these bars.

Now, which discharge characterizations do these bars apply to? The bars apply to the reasons for discharge, not directly to the characterization. But typically:
- Dishonorable discharge: usually for serious offenses like mutiny, spying, moral turpitude
- Bad-conduct discharge: for less serious offenses, often from special court-martial
- Other than honorable (OTH): administrative discharge for misconduct
- General (under honorable conditions): administrative discharge for minor issues
- Uncharacterized: entry-level separations, etc.

From the document: "Former SMs who do not receive an Honorable discharge from DoD are ineligible for the VA Education benefit." But other benefits may be available.

On page 32363: "For example, education assistance under the Montgomery GI Bill program or Post-9/11 GI Bill program is available only for periods of service resulting in an ``honorable'' discharge. See 38 U.S.C. 3011(a)(3)(B) and 3311(c)(1). Therefore, former SMs who do not receive an Honorable discharge from DoD are ineligible for the VA Education benefit."

But for other benefits (compensation, pension, healthcare), the COD determination matters.

Now, the question is specifically about which separations that DARB counts as "less than honorable" does VA's rule expand eligibility for.

DARB counts as "less than honorable": dismissals, general, OTH, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, dishonorable.

VA's rule expands eligibility primarily for those with OTH discharges who previously would have been barred by the regulatory bars for moral turpitude or willful and persistent misconduct, but now can qualify under the compelling circumstances exception.

Let me check if there's more specific information.

On page 32362: "Specifically, that the removal of the regulatory bars would undermine their ability to use the consequence of loss of VA benefits as a deterrent to misconduct. In addition, the Military Departments were concerned that removal of the ``in lieu of general court-martial'' bar would deprive the commander, or for covered offenses, Special Trial Counsel, of a tool to dispose of misconduct in an administrative forum while balancing the interests of justice and victim preferences."

This suggests the "in lieu of general court-martial" bar (which is the "discharge in lieu of trial" bar) was a concern, but VA decided NOT to apply the compelling circumstances exception to this bar.

So for discharges "in lieu of trial" (which would typically be OTH discharges given in lieu of court-martial), the compelling circumstances exception does NOT apply.

But for other OTH discharges (not in lieu of trial), and potentially for bad-conduct discharges, the compelling circumstances exception for moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct would apply.

Also, the document mentions: "VA is also not adopting the sexual acts bar from the proposed rule, as this misconduct will be sufficiently excluded by either the statutory bars or the remaining regulatory bars." And "VA agrees that the homosexual acts bar is outdated and unnecessary and is entirely removing this regulatory bar."

So the removal of the homosexual acts bar would help some with OTH or bad-conduct discharges related to homosexual acts.

Now, for the second part of the question: "from what discharge date can their holders seek that board's review?"

This refers to DARB review. From Document 5:

On page 94605: "Petitioners are eligible for a DARB review when all four criteria below are met:
The Service member's date of discharge or dismissal was on or after December 20, 2019;"

And footnote 30: "See 10 U.S.C. 1553a; see Public Law 116-92, section 523; see also DTM, ``DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board,'' published May 5, 2023, and is available on the DoD Directives Division website and can be accessed at https://www.esd.whs.mil/Directives/Recent-Publications/. If this portion of the rule is held to be invalid by a court, the remainder of the rule should be considered severable and not affected by such determination."

Also on page 94606: "Because section 116-92 of the FY 2020 NDAA and 10 U.S.C. 1553a became effective on December 20, 2019, the DARB requires that the Service member was discharged or dismissed on or after December 20, 2019, to be eligible for this additional review."

So the discharge date must be on or after December 20, 2019.

Now, to answer the first part precisely: which separations (discharge types) that DARB counts as "less than honorable" does VA's 2024 rule expand eligibility for?

From the VA rule (Document 3), the expansion comes from:
1. Removal of the homosexual acts regulatory bar
2. Addition of compelling circumstances exception for moral turpitude bar
3. Addition of compelling circumstances exception for willful and persistent misconduct bar
4. Clarification that regulatory bars only apply to conduct that formed the basis of discharge

These changes would primarily benefit those with:
- Other than honorable (OTH) discharges (for reasons other than "in lieu of trial")
- Bad-conduct discharges (BCD)
- Possibly general discharges, though these are less likely to have regulatory bars applied

But not for:
- Dishonorable discharges (statutory bar)
- Dismissals (for officers, similar to dishonorable)
- Discharges "in lieu of trial" (OTH in lieu of GCM - no compelling circumstances exception)
- Mutiny or spying (no compelling circumstances exception)

The document states on page 32362: "Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct."

And: "Based upon interagency concerns, VA has decided not to alter the current regulatory bar for MT and does not adopt the language from the proposed rule. This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

Ah, here it is! Explicitly mentions BCD and OTH discharges.

"while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

So the VA 2024 rule expands benefit eligibility specifically for those with Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) and Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges.

These are both categories that DARB counts as "less than honorable" (per footnote 42 in Document 5).

Now, for the discharge date for DARB review: December 20, 2019 or later.

Let me confirm both parts.

First part: VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expands benefit eligibility for holders of Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) and Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges (which are separations that DARB counts as "less than honorable").

Second part: Holders can seek DARB review if their discharge date was on or after December 20, 2019.

The question asks: "For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility, and from what discharge date can their holders seek that board's review?"

So the answer should be: Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) and Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges; December 20, 2019.

Let me make sure there aren't others. The document specifically calls out BCD and OTH. General discharges are "under honorable conditions" so they might not face the same bars, but the document doesn't explicitly say the rule expands eligibility for general discharges. Uncharacterized discharges are mentioned in Document 3 page 32370: "Per 38 CFR 3.12(k) (redesignated in this rule to Sec. 3.12(l)), there are three types of uncharacterized separations: (1) entry level separation; (2) void enlistment or induction; and (3) dropped from the rolls. An entry level separation is considered under conditions other than dishonorable; accordingly, this rulemaking does not apply to this type of uncharacterized separation." And "when a former SM was dropped from the rolls, the facts and circumstances surrounding the separation must be reviewed to determine whether the separation was under conditions other than dishonorable. These determinations are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking for the reasons discussed above."

So "dropped from the rolls" (a type of uncharacterized separation) is treated like OTH and would benefit. But the question asks for "separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable", and DARB counts uncharacterized as less than honorable (per footnote 42). However, the VA rule specifically highlights BCD and OTH as the ones getting the case-by-case analysis.

Given the explicit statement: "whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility," I think BCD and OTH are the primary answers.

Also, in the context of the question, it says "separations" plural, and BCD and OTH are two specific separation types.

For the date, it's clearly December 20, 2019.

Let me check if the VA rule has a specific name or date. Document 3 is dated April 26, 2024, and says "Effective date: This final rule is effective June 25, 2024." But the question says "VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule" which matches.

And for DARB, Document 5 is dated November 29, 2024, establishing the DARB rule.

So the answer should be: Bad-Conduct Discharges and Other Than Honorable discharges; December 20, 2019.

Full answer
-----------

The VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule (effective June 25, 2024) expands benefit eligibility specifically for Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) and Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges among the separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) counts as "less than honorable."

As stated in Document 3 (89 FR 32361-32373): "This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants 'veteran' status and VA benefits eligibility." The rule accomplishes this by removing the homosexual acts regulatory bar, refining the "willful and persistent misconduct" standard, and expanding the "compelling circumstances" exception to apply to both moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct bars.

Holders of these discharges can seek DARB review if their discharge date was on or after December 20, 2019. As specified in Document 5 (89 FR 94603-94613): "The Service member's date of discharge or dismissal was on or after December 20, 2019" is the first of four eligibility criteria for DARB review, with the eligibility date aligning with "the language in section 523 of the NDAA for FY 2020" which "became effective on December 20, 2019."

Reasoning
---------

Let me analyze this question carefully. The question is asking about two specific things:

1. For which separations (discharges) that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) counts as "less than honorable" does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility?

2. From what discharge date can their holders seek that board's review?

I need to find information about both the VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule and the DARB's eligibility requirements from the provided documents.

First, let me find information about the VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule. Looking at Document 3, which appears to be a Federal Register notice from April 26, 2024 (Volume 89, Issue 82) about "Update and Clarify Regulatory Bars to Benefits Based on Character of Discharge" from the Department of Veterans Affairs.

From Document 3:

"SUMMARY: In a document published in the Federal Register on July 10, 2020, the Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) proposed to amend its regulation regarding character of discharge (COD) determinations. After considering public comments, VA has decided to finalize its proposal with some modifications to expand VA benefits eligibility, bring more consistency to adjudications of benefits eligibility, and ensure COD determinations consider all pertinent factors.

DATES:
Effective date: This final rule is effective June 25, 2024.
Applicability date: The provisions of this final rule shall apply to all applications for benefits that are received by VA on or after June 25, 2024, or that are pending before VA, the United States Court of Appeals for Veterans Claims, or the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit (Federal Circuit) on June 25, 2024."

So this is the VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule, effective June 25, 2024.

Now, what does this rule do? Let me find where it explains what discharges it affects.

From Document 3:

"Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct. The definition for willful and persistent misconduct has been refined for more objective application, and an expanded compelling circumstances exception now applies to both the moral turpitude (MT) and willful and persistent misconduct bars."

And further:

"Based upon interagency concerns, VA has decided not to alter the current regulatory bar for MT and does not adopt the language from the proposed rule. This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

Also: "Removal of Homosexual Acts Bar" section states: "VA agrees that the homosexual acts bar is outdated and unnecessary and is entirely removing this regulatory bar. VA is also not adopting the sexual acts bar from the proposed rule, as this misconduct will be sufficiently excluded by either the statutory bars or the remaining regulatory bars."

So the VA's 2024 rule:
1. Removes the "homosexual acts" regulatory bar entirely
2. Keeps four regulatory bars: (1) discharge in lieu of GCM, (2) mutiny/spying, (3) moral turpitude, (4) willful and persistent misconduct
3. Refines the definition of "willful and persistent misconduct" for more objective application
4. Expands the "compelling circumstances" exception to apply to both moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct bars

The rule specifically mentions it will help "former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge" get benefits.

Now, what discharges does the DARB consider "less than honorable"? Let me check Document 5, which is about the DARB (Discharge Appeal Review Board).

From Document 5 (Federal Register Volume 89, Issue 230, November 29, 2024):

"Characterization of less than honorable. A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

And in the eligibility section:

"(2) Service member received a less than honorable characterization of service at the time of discharge or dismissal;"

Also, footnote 42 states: "A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

So the DARB considers these as "less than honorable":
- General (under honorable conditions)
- Other than honorable (OTH)
- Uncharacterized
- Bad-conduct discharge (BCD)
- Dishonorable discharge
- Dismissal

Now, which of these does the VA's 2024 rule expand eligibility for?

From Document 3, the VA rule specifically mentions helping those with "Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge". It also mentions removing the homosexual acts bar, which would have affected some OTH discharges.

The rule states: "This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

It also says: "Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d). That said, a favorable COD determination under this rule does not result in blanket eligibility for all VA benefits or a change in the Department of Defense's (DoD) discharge characterization. Rather, certain VA benefits have specific eligibility requirements as it pertains to COD. For example, education assistance under the Montgomery GI Bill program or Post-9/11 GI Bill program is available only for periods of service resulting in an ``honorable'' discharge."

Importantly: "Former SMs who do not receive an Honorable discharge from DoD are ineligible for the VA Education benefit."

But for other benefits, the rule expands eligibility for those with BCD or OTH discharges through the compelling circumstances exception.

The rule also mentions: "Moreover, while relaxing the bars to eligibility, this final rule does not extend VA benefits eligibility to all former SMs. Former SMs who do not meet the criteria for benefits eligibility may remain entitled to certain critical benefits to address the harms caused by their military service such as mental health and substance use care, emergent suicide care, and medical care in emergency situations, as discussed below."

And: "It is important to clarify here that the regulatory bars shall only be applied when they are clearly supported by the military record. The benefit of the doubt will be resolved in favor of the former SM."

Also: "Further, as discussed below, VA agrees with the commenters who recommended limiting the conduct being considered for a COD determination to only that which formed the basis of the discharge from service. In short, if the military decided that a SM's misconduct did not preclude the SM from continuing to serve, then it also should not preclude benefits eligibility. This limitation will prevent conduct unrelated to the basis of the discharge from contributing to a bar from benefits."

So the VA's 2024 rule expands benefit eligibility primarily for those with BCD (Bad-Conduct Discharge) and OTH (Other Than Honorable) discharges, by:
1. Removing the homosexual acts bar
2. Refining the willful and persistent misconduct bar
3. Expanding the compelling circumstances exception to apply to moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct bars

It does not appear to expand eligibility for dishonorable discharges or dismissals, as those are statutory bars (38 U.S.C. 5303(a)) that VA doesn't have authority to change.

Now for the second part of the question: "from what discharge date can their holders seek that board's review?"

This is asking about the DARB (Discharge Appeal Review Board), not the VA. So I need to find what discharge date makes someone eligible for DARB review.

From Document 5:

"Petitioners are eligible for a DARB review when all four criteria below are met:

The Service member's date of discharge or dismissal was on or after December 20, 2019;"

And footnote 30: "See 10 U.S.C. 1553a; see Public Law 116-92, section 523; see also DTM, ``DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board,'' published May 5, 2023, and is available on the DoD Directives Division website and can be accessed at https://www.esd.whs.mil/Directives/Recent-Publications/. If this portion of the rule is held to be invalid by a court, the remainder of the rule should be considered severable and not affected by such determination."

Also: "Because section 116-92 of the FY 2020 NDAA and 10 U.S.C. 1553a became effective on December 20, 2019, the DARB requires that the Service member was discharged or dismissed on or after December 20, 2019, to be eligible for this additional review. The eligibility date aligns with the language in section 523 of the NDAA for FY 2020 and allows for faster action while also affording finality to prior, and potentially long-resolved, Military Departments' decisions on requests for upgrades, which would in turn permit earlier intervention by a civilian court. Additionally, it provides for clear eligibility determinations by establishing an explicit date-certain timeframe for eligibility."

So the discharge date must be on or after December 20, 2019.

But the question asks: "from what discharge date can their holders seek that board's review?"

The "their" refers to the separations (discharges) that the VA's 2024 rule expands eligibility for - which we determined are BCD and OTH discharges.

So for holders of BCD and OTH discharges (which are among the "less than honorable" discharges that DARB reviews), they can seek DARB review if their discharge date was on or after December 20, 2019.

Let me double-check if there are any other restrictions.

From Document 5: "Petitioners are eligible for a DARB review when all four criteria below are met:
(1) The Service member's date of discharge or dismissal was on or after December 20, 2019;
(2) Service member received a less than honorable characterization of service at the time of discharge or dismissal;
(3) The petitioner has exhausted all remedies available at the respective Military Departments' DRB and BCM/NR; and
(4) The petitioner's request for an upgrade in the characterization of a discharge or dismissal was denied or it was only partially granted at their respective Military Department's BCM/NR."

So yes, the discharge date must be on or after December 20, 2019.

Now, to confirm: the VA's 2024 rule expands eligibility for BCD and OTH discharges (among the "less than honorable" discharges that DARB reviews), and holders of such discharges can seek DARB review if discharged on or after December 20, 2019.

Let me make sure I'm not missing anything. The question says: "For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility..."

From Document 3, the VA rule expands eligibility for those with BCD and OTH discharges specifically. It mentions: "whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

It also removed the homosexual acts bar, which would have affected some OTH discharges.

The rule does not appear to expand eligibility for general discharges (under honorable conditions), uncharacterized discharges, dishonorable discharges, or dismissals.

For dishonorable discharges and dismissals, Document 3 states: "those who received a Dishonorable discharge from the military are excluded from benefits eligibility" and "Former SMs who do not receive an Honorable discharge from DoD are ineligible for the VA Education benefit."

Also: "VA has determined (after several rounds of public input) that the current regulatory approach to SMs with BCD and OTH discharges needs a restructuring to strike the appropriate balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them, while also limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct."

So the VA's 2024 rule specifically targets expanding eligibility for BCD and OTH discharges.

And for DARB review, the discharge date must be on or after December 20, 2019.

So the answer should be: The VA's 2024 rule expands benefit eligibility for Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) and Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges, and holders of such discharges can seek DARB review if discharged on or after December 20, 2019.

Let me check if there's any mention of "general (under honorable conditions)" discharges being helped by the VA rule.

From Document 3: "a discharge under honorable conditions is binding on the Department of Veterans Affairs as to character of discharge." And "honorable or general (under honorable conditions) discharge" are mentioned as not requiring COD determination.

In the regulatory text at the end of Document 3: "If the former service member did not die in service, then pension, compensation, or dependency and indemnity compensation is payable for claims based on a period of service that was terminated by discharge or release under conditions other than dishonorable. (38 U.S.C. 101(2)) A discharge under honorable conditions is binding on the Department of Veterans Affairs as to character of discharge."

And in the statutory bars section, it lists various bars but general discharge isn't one of them.

Actually, "general (under honorable conditions)" is considered "under conditions other than dishonorable" so it shouldn't be a bar to benefits. The issue is with OTH, BCD, and dishonorable.

From 38 U.S.C. 101(2): "The term 'veteran' means a person who served in the active military, naval, air, or space service, and who was discharged or released therefrom under conditions other than dishonorable."

And "conditions other than dishonorable" has been interpreted to include honorable, general (under honorable conditions), but not OTH, BCD, or dishonorable.

But VA has regulatory authority to determine what "conditions other than dishonorable" means, which is what 38 CFR 3.12 does.

From Document 3: "Eligibility for most VA benefits requires that a former service member (SM) be a ``veteran.'' ``Veteran'' status is bestowed to former SMs ``who served in the active military, naval, air, or space service, and who [were] discharged or released therefrom under conditions other than dishonorable.'' 38 U.S.C. 101(2). The term ``conditions other than dishonorable'' is not a term of art in the military and was chosen by Congress in 1944 to provide VA some discretion with respect to setting the standard for Veteran status and benefits eligibility of former SMs."

So VA has discretion to determine which discharges count as "other than dishonorable."

Historically, VA has considered honorable and general (under honorable conditions) as "other than dishonorable," but OTH, BCD, and dishonorable as "dishonorable" for VA purposes, unless an exception applies.

The VA's 2024 rule is expanding the exceptions for OTH and BCD discharges.

It does not appear to be changing anything for general (under honorable conditions) discharges, as those were already considered "other than dishonorable."

Similarly, uncharacterized discharges (like entry level separations) are handled separately and are generally considered "other than dishonorable."

From Document 3: "Per 38 CFR 3.12(k) (redesignated in this rule to Sec. 3.12(l)), there are three types of uncharacterized separations: (1) entry level separation; (2) void enlistment or induction; and (3) dropped from the rolls. An entry level separation is considered under conditions other than dishonorable; accordingly, this rulemaking does not apply to this type of uncharacterized separation."

And for "dropped from the rolls": "when a former SM was dropped from the rolls, the facts and circumstances surrounding the separation must be reviewed to determine whether the separation was under conditions other than dishonorable. These determinations are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking for the reasons discussed above."

So "dropped from the rolls" is treated like OTH, so the rule would help those too. But "dropped from the rolls" is a type of uncharacterized separation, not a standard discharge characterization.

The standard discharge characterizations are: Honorable, General (under honorable conditions), Other Than Honorable (OTH), Bad Conduct (BCD), Dishonorable, and Dismissal (for officers).

Uncharacterized is for entry-level separations, etc.

So for the standard "less than honorable" discharges that DARB reviews (per Document 5: general, OTH, uncharacterized, BCD, dishonorable, dismissal), the VA rule expands eligibility for OTH and BCD specifically, and also for "dropped from the rolls" (a type of uncharacterized).

But the question asks about "separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable" - and DARB's definition includes "general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

Among these, the VA rule expands eligibility for:
- Other than honorable (OTH)
- Bad-conduct discharge (BCD)
- Possibly "uncharacterized" but only the "dropped from the rolls" subtype, which is treated as OTH

But primarily OTH and BCD.

The rule specifically calls out BCD and OTH multiple times.

For the discharge date for DARB review: December 20, 2019 or later.

So my final answer: The VA's 2024 rule expands benefit eligibility for Other Than Honorable (OTH) and Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) [and possibly "dropped from the rolls" uncharacterized separations], and holders can seek DARB review if discharged on or after December 20, 2019.

But to be precise based on the documents, the rule specifically highlights BCD and OTH.

Let me check one more time in Document 3 for explicit statement.

"This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

"Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d)."

"VA has determined (after several rounds of public input) that the current regulatory approach to SMs with BCD and OTH discharges needs a restructuring..."

So yes, specifically BCD and OTH discharges.

And for DARB eligibility date: "The Service member's date of discharge or dismissal was on or after December 20, 2019"

So the answer is: Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) and Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges; December 20, 2019.

Full answer

Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) and Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges; on or after December 20, 2019

Reasoning
---------

I need to analyze the question carefully and find the relevant information in the provided documents.

The question asks:
"For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility, and from what discharge date can their holders seek that board's review?"

So I need to find two pieces of information:
1. Which types of separations (discharge characterizations) that the DARB (Discharge Appeal Review Board) considers "less than honorable" have expanded benefit eligibility under VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule
2. From what discharge date can holders of these separations seek DARB review

Let me search through the documents for information about:
- DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB)
- VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule
- Types of discharges considered "less than honorable" by DARB
- Which of these have expanded eligibility under VA's 2024 rule
- The discharge date cutoff for DARB review eligibility

First, let me find information about the DARB and what it considers "less than honorable" discharges.

From Document 5 (Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 230, November 29, 2024), which discusses the DARB:

In the "Definitions" section (Sec. 73.2):
"Characterization of less than honorable. A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

Also, in Sec. 73.5(a) "Who is eligible for a final review?":
"(2) Service member received a less than honorable characterization of service at the time of discharge or dismissal;"

And footnote 42 explains: "A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

So the DARB considers these as "less than honorable":
- General (under honorable conditions)
- Other than honorable (OTH)
- Uncharacterized
- Bad-conduct discharge (BCD)
- Dishonorable discharge
- Dismissal

Now, what does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule say about expanding eligibility for these discharge types?

From Document 3:
"This final rule is effective June 25, 2024."
"Applicability date: The provisions of this final rule shall apply to all applications for benefits that are received by VA on or after June 25, 2024, or that are pending before VA, the United States Court of Appeals for Veterans Claims, or the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit (Federal Circuit) on June 25, 2024."

Let me find where it discusses which discharge types get expanded eligibility.

From Document 3:
"Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct."

And:
"Based upon interagency concerns, VA has decided not to alter the current regulatory bar for MT and does not adopt the language from the proposed rule. This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

Also:
"As indicated in its RFI, VA rigorously considered the possibility of making more sweeping liberalizing changes than finalized here. But as discussed throughout this notice, there is concern that more sweeping changes would reduce deterrents to misconduct in the military and undermine good order and discipline, as well as concerns that removal of the ``in lieu of general court-martial'' bar would deprive the commander, or for covered offenses, Special Trial Counsel, of a tool to dispose of misconduct in an administrative forum while balancing the interests of justice and victim preferences."

And importantly:
"Given those factors, with this rule, VA seeks to strike a balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them, even those whose service was not without blemish, and limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct."

Further:
"Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d). That said, a favorable COD determination under this rule does not result in blanket eligibility for all VA benefits or a change in the Department of Defense's (DoD) discharge characterization. Rather, certain VA benefits have specific eligibility requirements as it pertains to COD. For example, education assistance under the Montgomery GI Bill program or Post-9/11 GI Bill program is available only for periods of service resulting in an ``honorable'' discharge. See 38 U.S.C. 3011(a)(3)(B) and 3311(c)(1). Therefore, former SMs who do not receive an Honorable discharge from DoD are ineligible for the VA Education benefit."

Also:
"Moreover, while relaxing the bars to eligibility, this final rule does not extend VA benefits eligibility to all former SMs. Former SMs who do not meet the criteria for benefits eligibility may remain entitled to certain critical benefits to address the harms caused by their military service such as mental health and substance use care, emergent suicide care, and medical care in emergency situations, as discussed below."

Let me look for specific mention of which discharge types get expanded eligibility.

From Document 3:
"VA is also not adopting the sexual acts bar from the proposed rule, as this misconduct will be sufficiently excluded by either the statutory bars or the remaining regulatory bars."

And:
"VA agrees that the homosexual acts bar is outdated and unnecessary and is entirely removing this regulatory bar."

But more importantly:
"Based upon interagency concerns, VA has decided not to alter the current regulatory bar for MT and does not adopt the language from the proposed rule. This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

This seems key - it specifically mentions BCD and OTH discharges as getting expanded eligibility through the "compelling circumstances exception."

Let me confirm this.

From Document 3:
"The definition for willful and persistent misconduct has been refined for more objective application, and an expanded compelling circumstances exception now applies to both the moral turpitude (MT) and willful and persistent misconduct bars."

And:
"Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct."

Also:
"New paragraph (e) addresses the ``compelling circumstances'' exception. As noted above, new paragraphs (e)(1) through (3) expand upon current paragraphs (c)(6)(i) through (iii), with minor wording changes to reflect the fact that this language will now be applied to not just prolonged AWOL but also certain misconduct."

And in the regulatory text:
"(d) Regulatory bars to benefits. Benefits are not payable where the former service member was discharged or released under one of the conditions listed in paragraph (d)(1) or (2) of this section.
(1) Compelling circumstances exception is not applicable for:
(i) Discharge in lieu of trial. Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial.
(ii) Mutiny or espionage. Mutiny or spying.
(2) Compelling circumstances exception is applicable for:
(i) An offense involving moral turpitude. This paragraph (d)(2)(i) includes, generally, conviction of a felony.
(ii) Willful and persistent misconduct."

So the compelling circumstances exception (which expands eligibility) applies to:
- Moral turpitude offenses
- Willful and persistent misconduct

But does NOT apply to:
- Discharge in lieu of trial (OTH in lieu of GCM)
- Mutiny or spying

Now, which discharge characterizations correspond to these bars?

From earlier in Document 3:
"Currently, there are six ``statutory bars'' to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12. In addition, currently, there are five ``regulatory bars'' to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12, which states that discharges based on the five listed offenses are ``considered to have been issued under dishonorable conditions.'' The last update to Sec. 3.12(d) occurred in 1980, more than 40 years ago. The 1980 update provided examples of aggravated homosexual acts. 45 FR 2318 (Jan. 11, 1980)."

And:
"On July 10, 2020, VA published at 85 FR 41471 its proposal to amend its regulation governing COD determinations. Specifically, VA proposed to modify the regulatory standards for discharges considered ``dishonorable'' for VA benefit eligibility purposes, such as discharges due to ``willful and persistent misconduct,'' and ``homosexual acts involving aggravating circumstances or other factors affecting the performance of duty.''"

So the regulatory bars correspond to reasons for discharge that lead to "dishonorable conditions" characterization.

From the final rule, there are four regulatory bars, two of which (moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct) now have the compelling circumstances exception.

But what discharge characterizations do these correspond to?

Typically:
- Dishonorable discharge = statutory bar (from GCM)
- Bad Conduct Discharge (BCD) = from Special Court-Martial
- Other Than Honorable (OTH) = administrative discharge
- General (Under Honorable Conditions) = administrative discharge
- Honorable = administrative discharge

From Document 3: "a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

This suggests BCD and OTH are the ones getting expanded eligibility.

Also, in the discussion of the "discharge in lieu of trial" bar: "Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial."

This suggests that OTH discharges given in lieu of GCM are treated differently (no compelling circumstances exception).

Let me check Document 5 again for what discharges the DARB considers "less than honorable" and which of those might correspond to the bars with compelling circumstances exception.

From Document 5, Sec. 73.2: "Characterization of less than honorable. A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

And footnote 42: "A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

Now, which of these would be subject to the moral turpitude or willful and persistent misconduct bars (which now have compelling circumstances exception)?

Typically:
- Dishonorable discharge and dismissal are from General Court-Martial (statutory bars, not regulatory)
- Bad Conduct Discharge is from Special Court-Martial
- Other Than Honorable, General (Under Honorable Conditions), Uncharacterized are administrative discharges

Full answer
-----------

VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expands benefit eligibility for Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges and Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD), which are two categories DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) counts as "less than honorable" (per 32 CFR 73.2: "A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal"). The rule specifically "eliminat[es] one of the regulatory bars, refin[es] another, and appl[ies] a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned 'veteran' status" (89 FR 32363). Holders of such discharges dated on or after December 20, 2019 may seek DARB review, as "the DARB requires that the Service member was discharged or dismissed on or after December 20, 2019, to be eligible for this additional review" (89 FR 94606), consistent with 10 U.S.C. 1553a's effective date.

---------

Let me carefully analyze the question and the provided documents to find the answer.

The question asks:
1. For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility?

- DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) and what it considers "less than honorable" discharges

"Characterization of less than honorable. A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

This is in Section 73.2 Definitions.

Also, in Section 73.5 Application procedures:
"(a) Who is eligible for a final review? To be eligible for a final review, the following criteria must be met:
(1) The Service member's date of discharge or dismissal was on or after December 20, 2019;
(2) Service member received a less than honorable characterization of service at the time of discharge or dismissal;"

And footnote 42 states: "A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

So DARB considers the following as "less than honorable":
- General under honorable conditions
- Other than honorable conditions
- Uncharacterized
- Bad-conduct discharge
- Dishonorable discharge
- Dismissal

Now, let's look for VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule. Document 3 appears to be about VA's character of discharge rule from 2024.

Document 3 is titled: "Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 82 (Friday, April 26, 2024) - DEPARTMENT OF VETERANS AFFAIRS - 38 CFR Part 3 - RIN 2900-AQ95 - Update and Clarify Regulatory Bars to Benefits Based on Character of Discharge"

This looks like the VA's 2024 rule on character of discharge.

Let me find where it discusses expanding benefit eligibility for certain discharge types.

From Document 3:

"Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct. The definition for willful and persistent misconduct has been refined for more objective application, and an expanded compelling circumstances exception now applies to both the moral turpitude (MT) and willful and persistent misconduct bars."

Earlier in the document:
"Currently, there are six "statutory bars" to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12. In addition, currently, there are five "regulatory bars" to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12, which states that discharges based on the five listed offenses are "considered to have been issued under dishonorable conditions." The last update to Sec. 3.12(d) occurred in 1980, more than 40 years ago. The 1980 update provided examples of aggravated homosexual acts. 45 FR 2318 (Jan. 11, 1980)."

And:
"On July 10, 2020, VA published at 85 FR 41471 its proposal to amend its regulation governing COD determinations. Specifically, VA proposed to modify the regulatory standards for discharges considered "dishonorable" for VA benefit eligibility purposes, such as discharges due to "willful and persistent misconduct," and "homosexual acts involving aggravating circumstances or other factors affecting the performance of duty." VA also proposed to extend a "compelling circumstances" exception to certain regulatory bars to benefits to ensure consideration of all pertinent factors."

Further:
"VA agrees that the homosexual acts bar is outdated and unnecessary and is entirely removing this regulatory bar. VA is also not adopting the sexual acts bar from the proposed rule, as this misconduct will be sufficiently excluded by either the statutory bars or the remaining regulatory bars."

So VA's 2024 rule:
1. Removes the "homosexual acts" regulatory bar entirely
2. Refines the "willful and persistent misconduct" bar
3. Adds an expanded "compelling circumstances" exception that applies to both "moral turpitude" and "willful and persistent misconduct" bars
4. Keeps four regulatory bars: (1) discharge in lieu of GCM, (2) mutiny/spying, (3) moral turpitude, (4) willful and persistent misconduct

The document also states: "Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d)."

Now, which specific discharge types (that DARB considers "less than honorable") get expanded benefit eligibility under VA's 2024 rule?

The VA rule is about character of discharge determinations for benefit eligibility. The regulatory bars determine when a discharge is considered "dishonorable" for VA benefits purposes.

The key is that VA's rule modifies how it evaluates certain discharge characterizations, particularly for "other than honorable" (OTH) discharges and bad-conduct discharges (BCD), since honorable and general discharges are generally eligible.

From Document 3: "Moreover, while relaxing the bars to eligibility, this final rule does not extend VA benefits eligibility to all former SMs. Former SMs who do not meet the criteria for benefits eligibility may remain entitled to certain critical benefits to address the harms caused by their military service such as mental health and substance use care, emergent suicide care, and medical care in emergency situations, as discussed below."

And: "It is important to clarify here that the regulatory bars shall only be applied when they are clearly supported by the military record. The benefit of the doubt will be resolved in favor of the former SM."

Also: "Further, as discussed below, VA agrees with the commenters who recommended limiting the conduct being considered for a COD determination to only that which formed the basis of the discharge from service. In short, if the military decided that a SM's misconduct did not preclude the SM from continuing to serve, then it also should not preclude benefits eligibility. This limitation will prevent conduct unrelated to the basis of the discharge from contributing to a bar from benefits."

The document mentions: "Between October 1, 2019, and September 30, 2022, VA deemed SMs with OTH discharges eligible for healthcare or benefits or both more than 75% of the time."

This suggests the rule particularly affects OTH discharges.

Let me look for more specific information about which discharge types get expanded eligibility.

From Document 3: "Given those factors, with this rule, VA seeks to strike a balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them, even those whose service was not without blemish, and limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct."

And: "On the other hand, there are some SMs whose service, while not without blemish, was generally of benefit to this Nation and therefore have earned the status of "veteran" and the benefits to which veterans are entitled. There are also SMs who service to our nation placed them in high-risk situations which could lead to injuries or other circumstances that increase risk for behaviors or conduct that Military Commanders deem inappropriate."

This suggests the rule helps those with OTH and BCD discharges who had mitigating circumstances.

Now, for the second part of the question: "from what discharge date can their holders seek that board's review?"

From Document 5, Section 73.5(a)(1): "The Service member's date of discharge or dismissal was on or after December 20, 2019;"

And footnote 30: "See 10 U.S.C. 1553a; see Public Law 116-92, section 523; see also DTM, "DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board," published May 5, 2023, and is available on the DoD Directives Division website and can be accessed at https://www.esd.whs.mil/Directives/Recent-Publications/. If this portion of the rule is held to be invalid by a court, the remainder of the rule should be considered severable and not affected by such determination."

Also: "Because section 116-92 of the FY 2020 NDAA and 10 U.S.C. 1553a became effective on December 20, 2019, the DARB requires that the Service member was discharged or dismissed on or after December 20, 2019, to be eligible for this additional review."

So the discharge date must be on or after December 20, 2019.

Now, to answer the first part: which specific separations (that DARB counts as less than honorable) does VA's 2024 rule expand benefit eligibility for?

DARB counts these as less than honorable:
- General under honorable conditions
- Other than honorable conditions
- Uncharacterized
- Bad-conduct discharge
- Dishonorable discharge
- Dismissal

VA's 2024 rule expands eligibility primarily for those with "other than honorable" (OTH) discharges and bad-conduct discharges (BCD), by:
1. Removing the homosexual acts bar
2. Refining the willful and persistent misconduct bar
3. Adding compelling circumstances exception for moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct

The document states: "Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d)."

And: "Between October 1, 2019, and September 30, 2022, VA deemed SMs with OTH discharges eligible for healthcare or benefits or both more than 75% of the time."

Also: "VA has refined the willful and persistent misconduct bar, as well as implemented a compelling circumstances exception, to distinguish between serious misconduct worthy of a "dishonorable" determination and misconduct that is mitigated by the circumstances or outweighed by otherwise meritorious service. The aim is to provide benefits in the latter situation, but not the former."

The rule specifically mentions helping those with OTH and BCD discharges: "VA believes this final rule does so by eliminating one of the regulatory bars, refining another, and applying a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned "veteran" status."

So VA's 2024 rule expands benefit eligibility primarily for those with Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges and Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD), which are two categories that DARB counts as "less than honorable."

Let me confirm if there are others. The document also mentions "uncharacterized" discharges in Section IV: "However, when a former SM was dropped from the rolls, the facts and circumstances surrounding the separation must be reviewed to determine whether the separation was under conditions other than dishonorable. These determinations are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking for the reasons discussed above."

So uncharacterized discharges (specifically those "dropped from the rolls") are also impacted.

But the main focus seems to be on OTH and BCD discharges.

Let me check if general discharges are affected. The document says: "If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Therefore, VA does not bar former SMs simply because they have minor offenses in their record."

So general discharges are not subject to COD determination, meaning they're generally eligible. The rule changes wouldn't expand eligibility for them because they're already eligible.

Dishonorable discharges and dismissals are statutory bars that VA doesn't have authority to override (except for the insanity exception).

So the main discharge types that DARB counts as "less than honorable" for which VA's 2024 rule expands eligibility are:
1. Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges
2. Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD)
3. Uncharacterized discharges (specifically "dropped from the rolls" type)

But the document most prominently discusses OTH and BCD.

This final rule's revision of Sec. 3.12(d) attempts to strike that balance."

And: "VA believes this final rule does so by eliminating one of the regulatory bars, refining another, and applying a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned "veteran" status."

This is very clear - the rule specifically targets expanding eligibility for those with BCD and OTH discharges.

The document also mentions uncharacterized discharges (dropped from rolls) being treated like OTH.

So the answer to the first part should be: Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges and Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) [and possibly uncharacterized discharges of the "dropped from rolls" type].
