Full answer
-----------

Answer

Cross‑referencing the two documents

Document 5 (DoD interim final rule, 32 CFR part 73 — DARB) defines the separations it treats as "characterization of less than honorable" as: general (under honorable conditions), other than honorable (OTH), uncharacterized, bad‑conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal — and makes a petitioner eligible for DARB final review only if the discharge/dismissal date was on or after December 20, 2019 (plus receipt of a less‑than‑honorable characterization, exhaustion of DRB/BCMNR remedies, and denial or only partial grant at the BCM/NR).

Document 3 (VA final rule, 38 CFR 3.12 — Character of Discharge, effective June 25, 2024) expands eligibility only for separations reviewed as potentially "other than dishonorable" despite misconduct, i.e., it removes one regulatory bar (homosexual acts), refines another (willful and persistent misconduct), and adds a compelling‑circumstances exception to the moral turpitude and willful/persistent misconduct bars. VA expressly says a favorable COD ruling "does not result in blanket eligibility for all VA benefits" and that, e.g., Montgomery/Post‑9/11 GI Bill still require an Honorable discharge; and it states that persons given a Dishonorable discharge are excluded from benefits eligibility, that a General (under honorable conditions) discharge "is binding on the Department of Veterans Affairs as to character of discharge" (§ 3.12(a)) so no COD review applies, that dismissals by general court‑martial remain a statutory bar, and that entry‑level separations and void enlistments among uncharacterized separations are not affected (the rule expressly covers the "dropped from the rolls" type of uncharacterized separation, which is adjudicated like an OTH discharge).

The qualifying separations (complete list):

1. Discharge under other than honorable (OTH) conditions
2. Bad‑conduct discharge (BCD)
3. Uncharacterized separation — specifically the "dropped from the rolls" type (reviewed in the same manner as an OTH discharge)

Not included: general (under honorable conditions) discharges (already binding and favorable on VA), dishonorable discharges (excluded), dismissals (statutory bar), and uncharacterized entry‑level separations or void enlistments (not covered by the rule).

Discharge date for seeking DARB review: the service member's discharge or dismissal must be dated on or after December 20, 2019 (the effective date of section 523, FY2020 NDAA / 10 U.S.C. 1553a).

Reasoning
---------

We need to cross-reference Document 5 (DoD DARB rule) and Document 3 (VA character of discharge rule 2024).

Document 5 defines "Characterization of less than honorable": "A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

DARB eligibility: discharge date on or after December 20, 2019.

VA's 2024 COD rule (Document 3) expands benefit eligibility. The question: for which of these separations does VA's 2024 rule expand eligibility?

The VA rule applies to former SMs who received OTH discharges, BCD, and uncharacterized "dropped from the rolls" separations. Let's think.

The VA rule amends 38 CFR 3.12(d) regulatory bars. The bars apply where the former service member was discharged or released under conditions listed. The rule removes the homosexual acts bar entirely, refines willful and persistent misconduct, adds compelling circumstances exception to moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct bars.

Which characterizations does the rule expand eligibility for? The rule affects BCD and OTH discharges primarily. Let's check: "It allows VA to determine whether the misconduct leading to an OTH discharge actually rendered the service dishonorable..." Also "favorably impacted by this rulemaking" — the uncharacterized "dropped from the rolls" separations: "However, when a former SM was dropped from the rolls, the facts and circumstances surrounding the separation must be reviewed to determine whether the separation was under conditions other than dishonorable. These determinations are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking for the reasons discussed above."

Also entry level separation: "An entry level separation is considered under conditions other than dishonorable; accordingly, this rulemaking does not apply to this type of uncharacterized separation." Void enlistments reviewed under 38 CFR 3.14, not impacted.

So the DARB "less than honorable" list: general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, dismissal.

VA2024 rule expands eligibility for: OTH, BCD, and uncharacterized (dropped from the rolls). Dishonorable discharges: excluded — VA says "those who received a Dishonorable discharge from the military are excluded from benefits eligibility." General under honorable conditions: "A discharge under honorable conditions is binding on the Department of Veterans Affairs as to character of discharge" (38 CFR 3.12(a)) — so general discharges are eligible; no COD determination; the rule doesn't expand eligibility there (they're already eligible). Dismissal: dismissals pursuant to GCM — statutory bar (discharged or dismissed by general court-martial) — 38 U.S.C. 5303(a). Actually the statutory bar (c)(2) covers "discharged or dismissed by the sentence of a general court-martial." So dismissal by GCM is statutorily barred — VA rule doesn't change that.

Hmm, but what about dismissal? The regulatory bar (d)(1)(i) is "Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial." That's separate.

So the answer: Other than honorable (OTH) conditions discharges, bad-conduct discharges (BCD), and uncharacterized separations where the member was dropped from the rolls (reviewed as OTH). Excluded: general under honorable conditions (already eligible/binding), dishonorable (statutorily barred), dismissals (statutory bar), entry-level separations and void enlistments (not impacted).

Wait — but does the VA rule expand eligibility for BCD? Let's check. The regulatory bars apply to those with BCD or OTH. VA states: "this final rule precludes benefits eligibility for only those SMs who committed misconduct that renders their service effectively dishonorable." Also "allows VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants 'veteran' status and VA benefits eligibility."

Also, earlier: "VA recognizes that the Military Departments use the prospect of VA benefits bars as one tool... VA has decided not to remove all the regulatory bars, but to remove one and modify one. In that way, the changes in this final rule expand VA benefits to more SMs than ever before."

Also, the uncharacterized dropped from the rolls: "Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking."

Hmm, but also dishonorable discharges — VA says those are excluded. Actually, let's re-read: "It is true that character of service determinations remain DoD's responsibility... VA maintains its authority to determine COD for purposes of VA benefits eligibility." And "VA assures the commenters that those who received a Dishonorable discharge from the military are excluded from benefits eligibility." So dishonorable not expanded.

What about "dismissal"? A dismissal is only adjudged at a court-martial (officer equivalent of dishonorable discharge). Under 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) statutory bar (c)(1)? Actually (c)(2): "As a conscientious objector who refused..." Let's recall the six statutory bars in 5303(a): (1) discharged or dismissed by the sentence of a general court-martial; (2) conscientious objector...; (3) deserter; (4) officer resigned for good of the service; (5) refusal of duty after conscientious objection...; (6) AWOL >180 days. Actually (c)(6) in the reg is AWOL 180 days. So dismissal by GCM = statutory bar (c)(1) — not modified by rule (well, the compelling circumstances exception extends to AWOL 180 days statutory bar — (c)(6)(i)). So dismissal isn't expanded.

Hmm, but wait. Is there an argument that "dismissal" could occur administratively? Document 5 says Coast Guard issues discharges for officers: "honorable, general/under honorable conditions, OTH, dismissal pursuant to GCM or administrative separation." So dismissal can be pursuant to GCM or administrative separation. But the VA rule doesn't address dismissals specifically beyond statutory bars.

So the answer list: 
1. Other than honorable (OTH) discharges
2. Bad-conduct discharges (BCD)
3. Uncharacterized separations (specifically "dropped from the rolls" type)

And the discharge date: on or after December 20, 2019.

Let me double-check the VA2024 rule for uncharacterized: "Per 38 CFR 3.12(k) (redesignated in this rule to Sec. 3.12(l)), there are three types of uncharacterized separations: (1) entry level separation; (2) void enlistment or induction; and (3) dropped from the rolls. An entry level separation is considered under conditions other than dishonorable; accordingly, this rulemaking does not apply to this type of uncharacterized separation. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Void enlistments are reviewed under the factors listed in 38 CFR 3.14, and thus are also not impacted by this rulemaking. However, when a former SM was dropped from the rolls, the facts and circumstances surrounding the separation must be reviewed to determine whether the separation was under conditions other than dishonorable. These determinations are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking for the reasons discussed above."

So the answer: OTH discharges, BCD, and uncharacterized separations where dropped from the rolls.

Hmm, but should we also include "general under honorable conditions"? The DARB counts it as "less than honorable" but VA's rule doesn't expand eligibility for it — general discharges are "binding on VA as to character of discharge" and are eligible (38 CFR 3.12(a)). So not expanded.

Dishonorable: VA explicitly says excluded. Not expanded.

Dismissal: statutory bar. Not expanded. Hmm, but is a dismissal always by GCM? For officers, dismissal is adjudged at GCM. So no.

Actually, wait: the regulatory bar (d)(1)(i) is "Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial" — the "or its equivalent" includes undesirable discharges. That bar has no compelling circumstances exception. So no expansion.

Also note the removal of the homosexual acts bar — that could affect anyone with BCD/OTH. But specifically the removal benefits those whose discharge was based on homosexual acts. Still, the characterizations affected are OTH and BCD.

Thus final answer: OTH conditions discharges; bad-conduct discharges; and uncharacterized separations ("dropped from the rolls") — reviewable by DARB if discharged on or after December 20, 2019.

Full answer
-----------

Answer

From Document 4 (DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board, 32 CFR Part 73), § 73.2, the DARB's definition of "characterization of less than honorable" encompasses the following separations:

1. General (under honorable conditions)
2. Other than honorable conditions (OTH)
3. Uncharacterized (e.g., entry-level separation)
4. Bad-conduct discharge
5. Dishonorable discharge
6. A dismissal

These are the only separations that can ground a DARB petition (per § 73.5(a)(2)).

Discharge-date threshold: Per § 73.5(a)(1), the Service member's date of discharge or dismissal must have been on or after December 20, 2019 (the effective date of section 523 of the FY 2020 NDAA / 10 U.S.C. 1553a).

Regarding "VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule": None of the six input documents is a VA character-of-discharge rule or regulation (the VA documents provided address the 85/15 rule and the STEM Scholarship). The DARB interim final rule itself, however, notes that VA makes fact-specific eligibility determinations for benefits (disability compensation, home loans, VA medical care) for individuals discharged under other than honorable conditions, and that G.I. Bill educational benefits require an honorable characterization (so even general (under honorable conditions) does not qualify). The documents do not contain the text of a 2024 VA rule expanding eligibility, so a precise cross-reference of which DARB categories benefit from such an expansion cannot be fully determined from the provided materials.

In summary — the complete DARB list of "less than honorable" separations and the review date are:

| Separation type | DARB review available from | |---|---| | General (under honorable conditions) | December 20, 2019 | | Other than honorable conditions | December 20, 2019 | | Uncharacterized | December 20, 2019 | | Bad-conduct discharge | December 20, 2019 | | Dishonorable discharge | December 20, 2019 | | Dismissal | December 20, 2019 |

Full answer
-----------

Separations Where the VA 2024 COD Rule Expands Eligibility

Among the separation characterizations the Discharge Review Board (DRB) treats as "less than honorable" — Other Than Honorable (OTH), Bad Conduct Discharge (BCD), and Dishonorable — the VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule (Document 3) expands benefit eligibility for holders of:

1. Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges
2. Bad Conduct Discharges (BCDs)

(Dishonorable discharges remain statutorily barred under 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and are not expanded by the rule.)

The expansion comes from the rule's removal of the homosexual acts regulatory bar entirely, its addition of a compelling circumstances exception to the moral turpitude bar, and its refinement of the willful and persistent misconduct bar (including a compelling circumstances exception and objective persistence timeframes).

Date From Which Their Holders Can Seek DRB Review

Under 10 U.S.C. 1553(b), holders of these discharges may seek the Discharge Review Board's review within 15 years of the date of their discharge or release — i.e., from their date of discharge, with a 15-year window to file an application.

Full answer
-----------

Separations and Board Review Date

Based on Document 3 (VA's 2024 final rule amending 38 CFR 3.12, effective June 25, 2024), the separations that DoD's discharge review boards (referenced in the regulation as Discharge Review Boards under 10 U.S.C. 1553) characterize as less than honorable, and for which the rule expands VA benefit eligibility, are:

1. Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges — the rule entirely removes the homosexual-acts regulatory bar, refines the "willful and persistent misconduct" bar with objective time-based criteria, and adds a compelling-circumstances exception to the moral-turpitude and willful/persistent-misconduct bars.
2. Bad Conduct Discharge (BCD) — similarly benefited by the compelling-circumstances exception and the refined misconduct definition.
3. Undesirable discharges (historic characterization, no longer issued) — the rule explicitly addresses "the no longer used undesirable discharge" in its re-adjudication provisions, and these holders are benefited by the removal of the homosexual-acts bar and other liberalizations.

From what discharge date can their holders seek the board's review?

October 8, 1977. This is the dividing date established in the revised regulation: paragraph (g) governs Discharge Review Board upgrades issued prior to October 8, 1977 (setting aside both statutory and regulatory bars except 3.12(c)(2)), and paragraph (h) governs upgrades issued on or after October 8, 1977 (setting aside regulatory bars under paragraph (d), but not statutory bars under paragraph (c)). Upgrades from the board on or after this date can set aside the regulatory bars that the 2024 rule modifies, thereby enabling favorable VA character-of-discharge determinations.

Full answer
-----------

VA’s 2024 character-of-discharge rule expands eligibility for veterans with “other than honorable” (and similar) separations that DoD’s Discharge Appeal Review Board treats as less-than-honorable when they resulted from:

- mental health conditions (including PTSD) or traumatic brain injury;
- sexual assault or sexual harassment experienced during service; or
- sexual orientation or gender identity (e.g., Don’t Ask, Don’t Tell-related separations).

In these cases, VA will no longer treat the discharge as a bar to benefits even if DoD still classifies the separation as less than honorable and has not upgraded it.

Full answer
-----------

The DARB treats a separation as “less than honorable” when it is general (under honorable conditions), other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad‑conduct, dishonorable, or a dismissal (Doc 5, §73.2, “Characterization of less than honorable”).

VA’s 2024 COD rule (Doc 3, 38 CFR Part 3, effective June 25 2024) expands benefit eligibility for people in those categories except where the separation was:

- accepted in lieu of trial by general court‑martial, or
- for mutiny or spying

(those two bars remain absolute and get no “compelling circumstances” exception).

Specifically, the 2024 rule:

1. Removes the old regulatory bar for discharges based on homosexual acts (the former §3.12(d) homosexual‑acts provision is deleted). So OTH, BCD, dishonorable, dismissal, or uncharacterized separations that were previously barred solely for that reason are now potentially eligible.
2. Adds a “compelling circumstances” exception to the remaining conduct‑based bars for moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct (§3.12(d)(2) and §3.12(e)). That means a former service member with an OTH, bad‑conduct, dishonorable, dismissal, or uncharacterized discharge that was based on one of those two types of misconduct can now qualify for VA benefits if mitigating/compelling circumstances outweigh the misconduct.

In short, VA’s 2024 rule expands eligibility for other‑than‑honorable, bad‑conduct, dishonorable, dismissal, and uncharacterized separations (the ones DARB counts as less than honorable) when the underlying reason was homosexual conduct (bar removed) or moral turpitude/willful and persistent misconduct that is overcome by compelling circumstances—but not for separations taken in lieu of general court‑martial or for mutiny/espionage. The rule explicitly discusses this balance for BCD and OTH discharges (Doc 3, Supplementary Information, §II).

Reasoning
---------

So DARB counts these as less than honorable.

Document 3 (VA final rule, Update and Clarify Regulatory Bars to Benefits Based on Character of Discharge, 38 CFR Part 3, effective June 25, 2024) modifies regulatory bars. Need determine for which separations among those listed does the 2024 rule expand benefit eligibility compared to prior?

Also applies compelling circumstances exception to moral turpitude and willful/persistent misconduct, but not to discharge in lieu of GCM or mutiny/spying. So the expansion of benefit eligibility is for those previously barred by homosexual acts, and also for those with moral turpitude or willful/persistent misconduct if compelling circumstances apply. We need identify those separation characterizations that are "less than honorable" per DARB definition but are not among the retained regulatory bars that preclude benefits, or are now subject to compelling circumstances.

Specifically, prior to 2024, VA's regulatory bars in 38 CFR 3.12(d) listed five categories of discharges considered dishonorable for benefits:
(i) discharge in lieu of GCM
(ii) mutiny or spying
(iii) moral turpitude

Full answer
-----------

Based on the documents, DoD’s Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) counts the following as “less than honorable”: general (under honorable conditions), other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, and dismissal (Document 5, §73.2).

VA’s 2024 character-of-discharge final rule (Document 3, 89 FR 32361, Apr. 26, 2024; 38 CFR 3.12) expands benefit eligibility for former service members with those separations in three ways:

1. Homosexual acts bar removed entirely – The prior regulatory bar disqualifying discharges based on homosexual acts involving aggravating circumstances is eliminated. So separations (including other-than-honorable, bad-conduct, or dismissals) previously barred solely for that reason are no longer excluded.
2. Willful and persistent misconduct narrowed and excepted – The definition is now objective (minor misconduct within 2 years of each other, or serious misconduct within 5 years). More importantly, an expanded “compelling circumstances” exception (Document 3, §3.12(e)) now applies to this bar, allowing eligibility for otherwise disqualified separations (e.g., OTH, BCD, dishonorable, dismissal) when mental/cognitive impairment, trauma, coercion, hardship, or other mitigating factors are present.
3. Moral turpitude now subject to compelling circumstances – The bar for offenses involving moral turpitude (Document 3, §3.12(d)(2)(i)) remains, but the same compelling-circumstances exception (§3.12(e)) applies, expanding eligibility for affected separations.

The rule also applies that compelling-circumstances exception to the statutory prolonged-AWOL bar (180+ days) in §3.12(c)(6). By contrast, the bars for acceptance of discharge in lieu of general court-martial and for mutiny/espionage (§3.12(d)(1)) remain absolute with no exception.

So, among the DARB “less than honorable” categories, VA’s 2024 rule particularly expands eligibility for other-than-honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, dishonorable, and dismissal separations that involve willful/persistent misconduct, moral turpitude, or homosexual acts—while leaving untouched those involving discharge in lieu of court-martial or mutiny/espionage.

The old 38 CFR 3.12(d) (before 2024) listed:

(i) Moral turpitude
(ii) Willful and persistent misconduct

So the bar for homosexual acts is removed entirely. The bar for discharge in lieu of GCM and mutiny/espionage remains without exception. The bars for moral turpitude and willful/persistent misconduct remain but are now subject to a compelling circumstances exception.

- Separations involving homosexual acts (since the bar is removed)
- Separations involving moral turpitude (if compelling circumstances exist)
- Separations involving willful and persistent misconduct (if compelling circumstances exist, and also because the definition is refined)

Full answer
-----------

The DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) treats the following characterizations as “less than honorable”: general (under honorable conditions), other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, and dismissal (32 CFR 73.2).

VA’s 2024 final rule amending 38 CFR 3.12 does not change the statutory bars that apply to dishonorable discharges and to dismissals/discharge by sentence of a general court-martial (38 CFR 3.12(c)(2), (c)(6), (d)(1)(i)). So those remain fully barred.

But the rule does expand eligibility for the other DARB-listed “less than honorable” separations—principally other than honorable conditions, general (under honorable conditions), uncharacterized, and bad-conduct discharges (when not by general court-martial)—by modifying or removing the regulatory bars that previously made those discharges “dishonorable” for VA purposes:

- Homosexual-acts bar removed entirely (old 38 CFR 3.12(d)(5) eliminated; 38 CFR 3.12(d) now lists only four regulatory bars).
- “Willful and persistent misconduct” narrowed—it now applies only to specific patterns: minor misconduct within 2 years of each other, minor misconduct within 2 years of more serious misconduct, or serious misconduct within 5 years of each other (38 CFR 3.12(d)(2)(ii)).
- “Compelling circumstances” exception added for:
- Moral turpitude (38 CFR 3.12(d)(2)(i)),
- Willful and persistent misconduct (38 CFR 3.12(d)(2)(ii)), and
- The statutory prolonged AWOL (180+ days) bar (38 CFR 3.12(c)(6) and (e)).

So the 2024 rule expands VA benefit eligibility for service members separated with other than honorable, general (under honorable conditions), uncharacterized, and bad-conduct (non-GCM) characterizations—specifically those whose discharges were previously barred solely because of homosexual conduct, because of misconduct that does not meet the new “persistent” pattern/timeframe criteria, or because of moral turpitude/willful misconduct/prolonged AWOL where compelling mitigating circumstances exist. The statutory bars for dishonorable discharge, dismissal, discharge in lieu of general court-martial trial, mutiny/espionage, and desertion remain unchanged and are not affected by the expansion.

Full answer
-----------

Based on the documents, DoD’s Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) defines a “Characterization of less than honorable” as including: general (under honorable conditions), other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal (32 CFR § 73.2, Document 5).

VA’s 2024 character-of-discharge rule (88 FR 32361, Apr. 26, 2024; 38 CFR § 3.12, Document 3) expands benefit eligibility for individuals with those separation types—primarily other-than-honorable (OTH) conditions, but also bad-conduct discharges, dishonorable discharges, and dismissals to the extent they are not barred by the unchanged statutory provisions—when the separation was based on specific conduct now covered by a “compelling circumstances” exception or by the removal of a bar. Specifically, the 2024 rule expands eligibility for separations involving:

- Prolonged AWOL (180+ days) that resulted in an OTH discharge (38 CFR § 3.12(c)(6) and (e));
- Willful and persistent misconduct (38 CFR § 3.12(d)(2)(ii));
- An offense involving moral turpitude (generally a felony conviction) (38 CFR § 3.12(d)(2)(i)); and
- Homosexual acts—the previous regulatory bar for this was eliminated entirely.

The rule applies the compelling-circumstances framework (considering mental/cognitive impairment, physical health, combat-related hardship, sexual abuse/assault, duress, family obligations, age, education, and maturity) to the AWOL, moral-turpitude, and willful-misconduct bars.

However, the 2024 rule does not expand eligibility for separations that are:

- An acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions (or its equivalent) in lieu of trial by general court-martial; or
- Based on mutiny or spying (38 CFR § 3.12(d)(1)).

So, among the separations DARB counts as “less than honorable,” VA’s rule chiefly expands eligibility for other-than-honorable conditions (and equivalent separations) that stem from the modified conduct categories above—provided they were not discharges accepted in lieu of general court-martial and do not involve mutiny or spying—while leaving the statutory bar for court-martial dismissals/dishonorable discharges unchanged except for the AWOL provision.

Full answer
-----------

The DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) treats the following as “less than honorable” separations (32 CFR 73.2): general (under honorable conditions), other than honorable (OTH), uncharacterized, bad‑conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, and dismissal.

VA’s 2024 character‑of‑discharge rule (38 CFR 3.12, effective June 25 2024) expands benefit eligibility mainly for the OTH, bad‑conduct, dismissal, and uncharacterized (e.g., “dropped from the rolls”) separations that were previously barred under the old regulatory bars, because the rule:

- Removes the bar for discharges based on homosexual acts.
- Refines and limits the “willful and persistent misconduct” bar with objective timeframes (minor misconduct within 2 years; serious misconduct within 5 years).
- Adds a “compelling circumstances” exception to:
- prolonged AWOL of 180 days or more (38 CFR 3.12(c)(6)), and
- offenses involving moral turpitude and willful/persistent misconduct (38 CFR 3.12(d)(2)).

The rule does not expand eligibility for separations taken in lieu of general court‑martial trial or for mutiny/espionage—those bars remain with no exception (38 CFR 3.12(d)(1)). So, among the DARB “less than honorable” categories, the 2024 VA rule opens benefits primarily for OTH conditions, bad‑conduct discharges, dismissals, and uncharacterized separations treated as OTH when the underlying reason was the now‑removed or modified conduct (homosexual acts, willful/persistent misconduct, moral turpitude, or prolonged AWOL with mitigating circumstances), but not for those separated in lieu of court‑martial or for mutiny/espionage.

Full answer

Other than honorable discharges and bad conduct discharges

Reasoning
---------

Let me carefully analyze the question and the provided documents to find the answer.

The question asks: "For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility?"

I need to find:
1. What separations DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) counts as "less than honorable"
2. What VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule says about expanding benefit eligibility
3. Where these two intersect - which specific separations that DARB considers "less than honorable" now have expanded VA benefit eligibility under the 2024 rule

First, let me find information about DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) and what it considers "less than honorable" separations.

From Document 5 (Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 230, November 29, 2024), which discusses the DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board:

On page 94605-94606, it states:
"Petitioners are eligible for a DARB review when all four criteria below are met:
...
The Service member received a less than honorable characterization of service; [footnote 31]
...
Footnote 31: See 10 U.S.C. 1553a(c)(1)."

And then in footnote 42 on page 94606:
"A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

So according to DARB, "less than honorable" characterizations include:
- Dismissals
- General (under honorable conditions)
- Other than honorable (OTH)
- Uncharacterized
- Bad-conduct discharges
- Dishonorable discharges

Now, let me find information about VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule and what it expands.

Document 3 (Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 82, April 26, 2024) is titled "Update and Clarify Regulatory Bars to Benefits Based on Character of Discharge" and appears to be VA's 2024 rule.

From the SUMMARY section:
"In a document published in the Federal Register on July 10, 2020, the Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) proposed to amend its regulation regarding character of discharge (COD) determinations. After considering public comments, VA has decided to finalize its proposal with some modifications to expand VA benefits eligibility, bring more consistency to adjudications of benefits eligibility, and ensure COD determinations consider all pertinent factors."

From the DATES section:
"Effective date: This final rule is effective June 25, 2024.
Applicability date: The provisions of this final rule shall apply to all applications for benefits that are received by VA on or after June 25, 2024, or that are pending before VA, the United States Court of Appeals for Veterans Claims, or the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit (Federal Circuit) on June 25, 2024."

So this is indeed VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule.

Now, what specific changes does this rule make that expand benefit eligibility?

From the beginning of Document 3:
"Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct. The definition for willful and persistent misconduct has been refined for more objective application, and an expanded compelling circumstances exception now applies to both the moral turpitude (MT) and willful and persistent misconduct bars."

Earlier it mentions: "Currently, there are six 'statutory bars' to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12. In addition, currently, there are five 'regulatory bars' to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12, which states that discharges based on the five listed offenses are 'considered to have been issued under dishonorable conditions.' The last update to Sec. 3.12(d) occurred in 1980, more than 40 years ago. The 1980 update provided examples of aggravated homosexual acts."

And: "On July 10, 2020, VA published at 85 FR 41471 its proposal to amend its regulation governing COD determinations. Specifically, VA proposed to modify the regulatory standards for discharges considered 'dishonorable' for VA benefit eligibility purposes, such as discharges due to 'willful and persistent misconduct,' and 'homosexual acts involving aggravating circumstances or other factors affecting the performance of duty.' VA also proposed to extend a 'compelling circumstances' exception to certain regulatory bars to benefits to ensure consideration of all pertinent factors."

Further: "VA agrees that the homosexual acts bar is outdated and unnecessary and is entirely removing this regulatory bar. VA is also not adopting the sexual acts bar from the proposed rule, as this misconduct will be sufficiently excluded by either the statutory bars or the remaining regulatory bars."

So the key changes in the 2024 rule:
1. Reduced regulatory bars from 5 to 4 (removed the "homosexual acts" bar)
2. Refined the definition of "willful and persistent misconduct" for more objective application
3. Expanded the "compelling circumstances" exception to apply to both moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct bars

Now, the question is asking specifically about which separations that DARB counts as "less than honorable" now have expanded VA benefit eligibility under this 2024 rule.

DARB counts these as "less than honorable":
- Dismissals
- General (under honorable conditions)
- Other than honorable (OTH)
- Uncharacterized
- Bad-conduct discharges
- Dishonorable discharges

But VA's rule changes affect regulatory bars, not statutory bars. The statutory bars are set by Congress in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and cannot be changed by VA regulation. The regulatory bars are VA's own additions.

From Document 3: "Currently, there are six 'statutory bars' to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12. In addition, currently, there are five 'regulatory bars' to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12..."

And: "Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct."

The document also states: "Given those factors, with this rule, VA seeks to strike a balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them, even those whose service was not without blemish, and limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct."

Importantly: "It is important to clarify here that the regulatory bars shall only be applied when they are clearly supported by the military record. The benefit of the doubt will be resolved in favor of the former SM. See 38 U.S.C. 5107(b), 38 CFR 3.102. In other words, when there is insufficient evidence of the alleged misconduct, racial bias in the allegation, or an approximate balance of positive and negative evidence about the alleged misconduct, the bar shall not be applied."

And: "Further, as discussed below, VA agrees with the commenters who recommended limiting the conduct being considered for a COD determination to only that which formed the basis of the discharge from service. In short, if the military decided that a SM's misconduct did not preclude the SM from continuing to serve, then it also should not preclude benefits eligibility. This limitation will prevent conduct unrelated to the basis of the discharge from contributing to a bar from benefits."

Also: "Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d). That said, a favorable COD determination under this rule does not result in blanket eligibility for all VA benefits or a change in the Department of Defense's (DoD) discharge characterization. Rather, certain VA benefits have specific eligibility requirements as it pertains to COD. For example, education assistance under the Montgomery GI Bill program or Post-9/11 GI Bill program is available only for periods of service resulting in an 'honorable' discharge. See 38 U.S.C. 3011(a)(3)(B) and 3311(c)(1). Therefore, former SMs who do not receive an Honorable discharge from DoD are ineligible for the VA Education benefit."

This last part is crucial - it says that for education benefits (GI Bill), you still need an Honorable discharge. But for other benefits, the rules have changed.

Let me look for more specific information about which discharge characterizations are affected.

From Document 3: "Between October 1, 2019, and September 30, 2022, VA deemed SMs with OTH discharges eligible for healthcare or benefits or both more than 75% of the time."

And: "VA is providing the documentation for this data in the rulemaking record."

Also: "VA thanks the commenter for their comment. VA believes that, through the modifications of this final rule, including the compelling circumstances exception, it will be able to expand VA benefits eligibility for former SMs with OTH discharges."

This suggests the rule particularly expands eligibility for those with OTH (Other Than Honorable) discharges.

Let me check if there's more specific information.

From the section "Removal of Homosexual Acts Bar":
"VA agrees that the homosexual acts bar is outdated and unnecessary and is entirely removing this regulatory bar. VA is also not adopting the sexual acts bar from the proposed rule, as this misconduct will be sufficiently excluded by either the statutory bars or the remaining regulatory bars."

This would affect people discharged for homosexual acts, who would often have received OTH discharges.

From the section "COD Process/Eligibility":
"Another commenter asked VA to presume eligibility for all SMs with administrative discharges except discharge in lieu of CM and stated that 'VA annually deems about 80 to 90 percent of veterans who received OTH have served `dishonorably'.' VA thanks the commenter for the comment, but that statistic is inaccurate. Between October 1, 2019, and September 30, 2022, VA deemed SMs with OTH discharges eligible for healthcare or benefits or both more than 75% of the time."

This again focuses on OTH discharges.

From the section "Compelling Circumstances":
"VA's intent with the compelling circumstances exception to the moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct bars is to provide claims processors a holistic means to evaluate the misconduct underlying a SM's discharge and to determine if that misconduct is outweighed by otherwise honorable service or can be excused due to circumstances influencing the former SM's decision-making around the time of the offense or otherwise providing context for the offense."

And: "Moreover, the compelling circumstances exception is designed to counter the possibility that certain military branches may have favored particular types of discharges during particular periods of time, including different periods of war. It allows VA to determine whether the misconduct leading to an OTH discharge actually rendered the service dishonorable, or alternatively was outweighed by otherwise honorable service or mitigated by the circumstances."

This specifically mentions OTH discharges.

From the section "Acceptance of an Undesirable Discharge To Escape General Court-Martial":
"One commenter opined that the regulatory bar associated with discharge in lieu of GCM should be clarified. The commenter went on to state that even though 'undesirable' is not used anymore as a discharge characterization, there are still some living veterans with 'undesirable' discharges that should not be excluded."

But it says: "VA thanks the commenters for their comments. Per the plain language of revised Sec. 3.12(d)(1)(i), this regulatory bar requires accepting an OTH discharge in lieu of trial by GCM..."

So this bar relates to OTH discharges accepted in lieu of court-martial.

From the section "Moral Turpitude":
"Given that the definition of moral turpitude under VAOPGC 6-87 requires a willful act that gravely violates accepted moral standards, it is difficult to imagine that minor misconduct--misconduct for which the maximum punishment is not longer than one year confinement--could ever meet that definition."

This suggests the moral turpitude bar would apply to more serious misconduct.

From the section "Willful and Persistent Misconduct":
"Overall, the proposed rule (and this final rule) brings both objectivity and liberalization to the 'willful and persistent misconduct' standard. The bar only applies if there are (1) instances of minor misconduct (as defined in reference to the MCM) occurring within two years of each other; (2) an instance of minor misconduct occurring within two years of more serious misconduct; or (3) instances of more serious misconduct occurring within five years of each other. Moreover, the compelling circumstances exception applies to this bar, such that even SMs whose misconduct meets the definition of 'willful and persistent' will receive an individualized review that considers whether the misconduct should be considered mitigated or outweighed by otherwise meritorious service or other factors."

And: "VA clarifies that, even though it uses the term 'minor' to distinguish one type of misconduct from another, this regulatory bar applies only to former SMs who have not received an Honorable or General (under honorable conditions) discharge. If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Therefore, VA does not bar former SMs simply because they have minor offenses in their record. And even for SMs with a BCD or OTH discharge, VA will not bar benefits for sporadic, minor misconduct, given the definition of 'persistent' in this final rule."

This is very important! It states that the willful and persistent misconduct bar (one of the regulatory bars) "applies only to former SMs who have not received an Honorable or General (under honorable conditions) discharge." So it applies to those with OTH, Bad Conduct, Dishonorable, etc. discharges, but NOT to those with General (under honorable conditions) discharges.

Also: "And even for SMs with a BCD or OTH discharge, VA will not bar benefits for sporadic, minor misconduct, given the definition of 'persistent' in this final rule."

So the rule changes particularly help those with OTH and Bad Conduct Discharges (BCD), by:
1. Removing the homosexual acts bar (which often led to OTH discharges)
2. Refining the willful and persistent misconduct bar to be more objective and only apply to persistent misconduct
3. Adding a compelling circumstances exception that can override the moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct bars

But it does NOT help those with Dishonorable discharges (which are statutory bars) or those with General (under honorable conditions) discharges (who weren't subject to these regulatory bars anyway).

Let me check Document 3 again for the list of regulatory bars before and after.

Before: 5 regulatory bars
After: 4 regulatory bars (removed homosexual acts bar)

The remaining 4 regulatory bars are:
1. Acceptance of discharge under OTH conditions in lieu of GCM
2. Mutiny or spying
3. Moral turpitude
4. Willful and persistent misconduct

And the compelling circumstances exception now applies to bars 3 and 4.

Now, which discharge characterizations do these regulatory bars apply to?

From the text: "the regulatory bars apply to the conditions under which 'the former service member was discharged or released' and VA affirms that this language means that only misconduct that led to the discharge may be considered in the COD determination."

And: "VA clarifies that, even though it uses the term 'minor' to distinguish one type of misconduct from another, this regulatory bar applies only to former SMs who have not received an Honorable or General (under honorable conditions) discharge."

So regulatory bars apply to those with less than General (under honorable conditions) discharges, i.e., OTH, BCD, Dishonorable, etc.

But Dishonorable discharges are statutory bars (38 U.S.C. 5303(a)), not regulatory bars. Let me confirm.

From Document 3: "Currently, there are six 'statutory bars' to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12."

And footnote: "38 U.S.C. 5303(a) lists the statutory bars."

Looking at 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) (not in the document but referenced), it typically includes things like dishonorable discharge, dismissal, etc.

Actually, from Document 3: "In October 1946, VA codified 38 CFR 2.1064, which reiterated that, for a former SM to obtain benefits, the SM must have been terminated under conditions 'other than dishonorable.' VA provided that 'dishonorable' discharges included those due to (1) mutiny; (2) spying; or (3) an offense involving moral turpitude or willful and persistent misconduct (terms that originated in Public Law 68-242, section 23, 43 Stat. 613 (1924)). 38 CFR 2.1064(a). And: "Currently, there are six 'statutory bars' to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12."

So statutory bars include things like dishonorable discharge, dismissal by GCM, etc.

Regulatory bars are VA's own additions beyond the statutory bars.

The document says: "In addition, currently, there are five 'regulatory bars' to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12, which states that discharges based on the five listed offenses are 'considered to have been issued under dishonorable conditions.'"

So regulatory bars are for discharges that aren't statutory bars but VA considers "dishonorable conditions" anyway.

Full answer
-----------

Based on the provided documents, VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule (Document 3, Federal Register Vol. 89, No. 82, April 26, 2024) expands benefit eligibility for two specific separation categories that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) classifies as "less than honorable":

1. Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges - Document 3 explicitly states: "VA has determined (after several rounds of public input) that the current regulatory approach to SMs with BCD and OTH discharges needs a restructuring to strike the appropriate balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them, while also limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct. This final rule's revision of Sec. 3.12(d) attempts to strike that balance."
2. Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD) - The same passage specifically identifies "BCD and OTH discharges" as the focus of the rule's expanded eligibility provisions. The rule implements "a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars [moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct], which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned 'veteran' status."

The rule does not expand eligibility for other DARB "less than honorable" categories:

- General (under honorable conditions) discharges don't undergo COD determinations at all (38 CFR 3.12(a))
- Dishonorable discharges and dismissals remain fully excluded ("those who received a Dishonorable discharge from the military are excluded from benefits eligibility")
- Uncharacterized separations only receive expanded eligibility for the "dropped from the rolls" subtype (treated like OTH), not entry-level separations or void enlistments

The expansion comes specifically from the new compelling circumstances exception (38 CFR 3.12(e)) that now applies to moral turpitude and willful/persistent misconduct regulatory bars - the primary bars affecting OTH and BCD determinations.

Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges and Bad-Conduct Discharges (BCD)

Let me analyze the question carefully. The question is asking: "For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility?"

Let me search through the documents for relevant information.

From Document 5 (Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 230, November 29, 2024), which discusses the DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board:

In the "Definitions" section (Sec. 73.2), it states:
"Characterization of less than honorable. A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

Also, in the eligibility criteria for DARB review (Sec. 73.5(a)(2)):
"Service member received a less than honorable characterization of service at the time of discharge or dismissal;"

And footnote 42 explains:
"A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

So DARB considers these as "less than honorable":
1. General (under honorable conditions)
2. Other than honorable (OTH)
3. Uncharacterized
4. Bad-conduct discharge (BCD)
5. Dishonorable discharge
6. Dismissal

Now, let's look at VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule. Document 3 appears to be about this - "Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 82 (Friday, April 26, 2024)" with the title "Update and Clarify Regulatory Bars to Benefits Based on Character of Discharge".

From Document 3:

"This final rule is effective June 25, 2024."

And in the SUMMARY:
"After considering public comments, VA has decided to finalize its proposal with some modifications to expand VA benefits eligibility, bring more consistency to adjudications of benefits eligibility, and ensure COD determinations consider all pertinent factors."

Let me find specifically what changes were made to expand eligibility.

From Document 3:
"Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct. The definition for willful and persistent misconduct has been refined for more objective application, and an expanded compelling circumstances exception now applies to both the moral turpitude (MT) and willful and persistent misconduct bars."

Also: "Based upon interagency concerns, VA has decided not to alter the current regulatory bar for MT and does not adopt the language from the proposed rule. This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants "veteran" status and VA benefits eligibility."

And: "Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d)."

Further: "It is important to clarify here that the regulatory bars shall only be applied when they are clearly supported by the military record. The benefit of the doubt will be resolved in favor of the former SM."

Also: "Further, as discussed below, VA agrees with the commenters who recommended limiting the conduct being considered for a COD determination to only that which formed the basis of the discharge from service. In short, if the military decided that a SM's misconduct did not preclude the SM from continuing to serve, then it also should not preclude benefits eligibility. This limitation will prevent conduct unrelated to the basis of the discharge from contributing to a bar from benefits."

And: "Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d). That said, a favorable COD determination under this rule does not result in blanket eligibility for all VA benefits or a change in the Department of Defense's (DoD) discharge characterization. Rather, certain VA benefits have specific eligibility requirements as it pertains to COD. For example, education assistance under the Montgomery GI Bill program or Post-9/11 GI Bill program is available only for periods of service resulting in an "honorable" discharge."

"VA has decided not to extend the compelling circumstances exception beyond the scope laid out in the proposed rule." regarding discharge in lieu of GCM.

But for moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct, there's an expanded compelling circumstances exception.

Also: "VA is also not adopting the sexual acts bar from the proposed rule, as this misconduct will be sufficiently excluded by either the statutory bars or the remaining regulatory bars."

And: "VA agrees that the homosexual acts bar is outdated and unnecessary and is entirely removing this regulatory bar."

Let me check the specific regulatory bars that remain and which ones were removed.

From Document 3:
"Currently, there are six "statutory bars" to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12. In addition, currently, there are five "regulatory bars" to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12, which states that discharges based on the five listed offenses are "considered to have been issued under dishonorable conditions." The last update to Sec. 3.12(d) occurred in 1980, more than 40 years ago. The 1980 update provided examples of aggravated homosexual acts. 45 FR 2318 (Jan. 11, 1980)."

Specifically, VA proposed to modify the regulatory standards for discharges considered "dishonorable" for VA benefit eligibility purposes, such as discharges due to "willful and persistent misconduct," and "homosexual acts involving aggravating circumstances or other factors affecting the performance of duty." VA also proposed to extend a "compelling circumstances" exception to certain regulatory bars to benefits to ensure consideration of all pertinent factors."

Then: "Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct."

So originally there were 5 regulatory bars, now there are 4. The one removed was the "homosexual acts" bar.

But the question is about which separations that DARB counts as "less than honorable" have expanded benefit eligibility under VA's 2024 rule.

DARB counts these as less than honorable:
1. General (under honorable conditions)
2. Other than honorable (OTH)
3. Uncharacterized
4. Bad-conduct discharge (BCD)
5. Dishonorable discharge

Also: "Moreover, while relaxing the bars to eligibility, this final rule does not extend VA benefits eligibility to all former SMs. Former SMs who do not meet the criteria for benefits eligibility may remain entitled to certain critical benefits to address the harms caused by their military service such as mental health and substance use care, emergent suicide care, and medical care in emergency situations, as discussed below."

Let me look for more specific information about which discharge types are affected.

From Document 3:
"This final rule precludes benefits eligibility for only those SMs who committed misconduct that renders their service effectively dishonorable."

And: "VA has determined (after several rounds of public input) that the current regulatory approach to SMs with BCD and OTH discharges needs a restructuring to strike the appropriate balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them, while also limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct. This final rule's revision of Sec. 3.12(d) attempts to strike that balance."

Also: "VA believes this final rule does so by eliminating one of the regulatory bars, refining another, and applying a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned "veteran" status."

This suggests that the main focus of the expansion is for BCD and OTH discharges.

Let me check the regulatory text in Document 3 to see the specific changes.

In the amended Sec. 3.12(d):
"(d) Regulatory bars to benefits. Benefits are not payable where the former service member was discharged or released under one of the conditions listed in paragraph (d)(1) or (2) of this section.
(1) Compelling circumstances exception is not applicable for:
(i) Discharge in lieu of trial. Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial.
(ii) Mutiny or espionage. Mutiny or spying.
(2) Compelling circumstances exception is applicable for:
(i) An offense involving moral turpitude. This paragraph (d)(2)(i) includes, generally, conviction of a felony.
(ii) Willful and persistent misconduct. For purposes of this section, instances of minor misconduct occurring within two years of each other are persistent; an instance of minor misconduct occurring within two years of more serious misconduct is persistent; and instances of more serious misconduct occurring within five years of each other are persistent. For purposes of this section, minor misconduct is misconduct for which the maximum sentence imposable pursuant to the Manual for Courts-Martial United States would not include a dishonorable discharge or confinement for longer than one year if tried by general court-martial."

And the compelling circumstances exception in (e) applies to (c)(6) [prolonged AWOL] and (d)(2) [moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct].

Now, which discharge characterizations correspond to these bars?

Typically:
- Dishonorable discharge and dismissal are punitive discharges from court-martial (general court-martial for dishonorable, special or general for dismissal of officers)
- Bad-conduct discharge is from special court-martial
- Other than honorable (OTH) is administrative
- General (under honorable conditions) is administrative
- Uncharacterized is typically entry-level separation

The regulatory bars in (d)(1) - discharge in lieu of GCM and mutiny/spying - would typically lead to OTH or possibly BCD.

The regulatory bars in (d)(2) - moral turpitude and willful/persistent misconduct - could lead to various discharge types including OTH, BCD, possibly general.

But the key expansion is that for moral turpitude and willful/persistent misconduct (which can lead to OTH and BCD discharges), there's now a compelling circumstances exception that can override the bar.

Whereas for discharge in lieu of GCM and mutiny/spying, there's no compelling circumstances exception.

Also, the homosexual acts bar was removed entirely.

Let me see if I can find more direct statement about which discharge types have expanded eligibility.

From Document 3:
"VA has determined (after several rounds of public input) that the current regulatory approach to SMs with BCD and OTH discharges needs a restructuring to strike the appropriate balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them, while also limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct."

And: "This final rule's revision of Sec. 3.12(d) attempts to strike that balance."

Also: "VA believes this final rule does so by eliminating one of the regulatory bars, refining another, and applying a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned "veteran" status."

This strongly suggests that the expanded eligibility primarily applies to those with BCD and OTH discharges.

Let me check if there's mention of other discharge types.

Regarding uncharacterized discharges, Document 3 states in Section IV:
"Per 38 CFR 3.12(k) (redesignated in this rule to Sec. 3.12(l)), there are three types of uncharacterized separations: (1) entry level separation; (2) void enlistment or induction; and (3) dropped from the rolls. An entry level separation is considered under conditions other than dishonorable; accordingly, this rulemaking does not apply to this type of uncharacterized separation. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Void enlistments are reviewed under the factors listed in 38 CFR 3.14, and thus are also not impacted by this rulemaking.
However, when a former SM was dropped from the rolls, the facts and circumstances surrounding the separation must be reviewed to determine whether the separation was under conditions other than dishonorable. These determinations are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking for the reasons discussed above."

So "dropped from the rolls" (a type of uncharacterized separation) is treated like OTH and thus benefits from the rule changes.

But entry-level separations and void enlistments are not affected.

For general (under honorable conditions) discharges, Document 3 states: "If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a)."

So general discharges are not subject to COD determination at all - they're automatically considered "under conditions other than dishonorable."

For dishonorable discharges and dismissals, Document 3 states: "VA thanks the commenters for their comments and assures the commenters that those who received a Dishonorable discharge from the military are excluded from benefits eligibility."

So dishonorable discharges and dismissals (for officers) remain fully barred.

Let me confirm this.

From Document 3: "That said, VA has determined (after several rounds of public input) that the current regulatory approach to SMs with BCD and OTH discharges needs a restructuring..."

And: "VA believes this final rule does so by eliminating one of the regulatory bars, refining another, and applying a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned "veteran" status."

Also: "One commenter stated that ``[c]hanges to VA shouldn't be bureaucratic, they should be legislative. In addition, Veterans should serve honorably throughout their contract otherwise they shouldn't be entitled to VA benefits.'' VA thanks the commenter for their comment. As discussed above, Congress delegated to VA the ability to set criteria for what constitutes "other than dishonorable" service for purposes of VA benefits eligibility. This rulemaking is necessary to refine those criteria. VA makes no changes to the regulatory text based on this comment.

One commenter stated ``I do not believe that anyone who receives a bad conduct or dishonorable discharge deserves to be treated by VA. Veterans wait forever for appointments and it's not right to add another million people to the rolls. We, honorable veterans, will never be seen. The VA needs to improve its track record before starting to reclassify people. The VA needs a lot more doctors and a lot more hospitals already.'' Another added that ``the added patient workload will also adversely impact the availability and timeliness of care received by all veterans at VA health care facilities.'' VA thanks the commenters for their comments and assures the commenters that those who received a Dishonorable discharge from the military are excluded from benefits eligibility."

This confirms dishonorable discharges remain excluded.

So to summarize what I've found:

DARB considers these as "less than honorable":
1. General (under honorable conditions) - but VA doesn't do COD determination for these; they're automatically eligible for most benefits
2. Other than honorable (OTH) - expanded eligibility under new rule via compelling circumstances exception for moral turpitude and willful/persistent misconduct bars
3. Uncharacterized (specifically "dropped from the rolls" type) - treated like OTH, so expanded eligibility
4. Bad-conduct discharge (BCD) - expanded eligibility under new rule via compelling circumstances exception
5. Dishonorable discharge - no expanded eligibility, remains barred
6. Dismissal (for officers) - same as dishonorable, remains barred

The question asks: "For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility?"

Based on the documents, the answer should be: Other than honorable (OTH) discharges, bad-conduct discharges (BCD), and uncharacterized discharges of the "dropped from the rolls" type.

But let me make sure about the "uncharacterized" part. DARB's definition includes "uncharacterized" as less than honorable, but VA treats different types of uncharacterized separations differently. Only "dropped from the rolls" gets the expanded eligibility; entry-level separations and void enlistments don't.

Full answer
-----------

Based on the provided documents, VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expands benefit eligibility for two specific separation characterizations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) classifies as "less than honorable":

1. Other than honorable conditions (referred to as "Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge" in VA's rule)
2. Bad-conduct discharge (referred to as "Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD)" in VA's rule)

This is explicitly stated in Document 3 (VA's 2024 final rule), where it states: "This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants 'veteran' status and VA benefits eligibility."

The rule further confirms: "VA believes that, through the modifications of this final rule, including the compelling circumstances exception, it will be able to expand VA benefits eligibility for former SMs with OTH discharges" and that the amendments "will increase the number of former SMs eligible for benefits" specifically for those with BCD or OTH discharges.

Document 5 (DARB rule) defines "Characterization of less than honorable" as including "a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal." However, VA's 2024 rule only expands eligibility for the "other than honorable conditions" and "bad-conduct discharge" characterizations from this list, as:

- General under honorable conditions doesn't require COD determination (38 CFR 3.12(a))
- Dishonorable discharge and dismissal are statutory bars (38 U.S.C. 5303(a))
- Uncharacterized separations only receive expanded eligibility for the "dropped from the rolls" subtype (treated like OTH), not the entire characterization

\boxed{\text{other than honorable conditions and bad-conduct discharge}}
