---------

Let me analyze this question carefully. The question is asking: "For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility?"

I need to find information about:
1. What separations DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) counts as "less than honorable"
2. What VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule does regarding benefit eligibility for those separations
3. Specifically which of those separations have expanded benefit eligibility under VA's 2024 rule

Let me search through the documents for relevant information.

First, let's find information about DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) and what it considers "less than honorable" discharges.

From Document 5 (Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 230, November 29, 2024), which discusses the DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board:

In the definitions section (Sec. 73.2), it states:
"Characterization of less than honorable. A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

Also, in Sec. 73.5(a)(2): "Service member received a less than honorable characterization of service at the time of discharge or dismissal;"

And footnote 42 explains: "A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

So according to DoD's DARB, "less than honorable" includes:
- General (under honorable conditions)
- Other than honorable (OTH)
- Uncharacterized
- Bad-conduct discharge (BCD)
- Dishonorable discharge
- Dismissal

Now, let's look at VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule. Document 3 appears to be about VA's 2024 rule on character of discharge determinations.

Document 3 is titled: "DEPARTMENT OF VETERANS AFFAIRS 38 CFR Part 3 RIN 2900-AQ95 Update and Clarify Regulatory Bars to Benefits Based on Character of Discharge" and is dated April 26, 2024 (Federal Register Volume 89 Issue 82).

Let me find where it discusses which discharges get expanded benefit eligibility.

In the SUMMARY section: "After considering public comments, VA has decided to finalize its proposal with some modifications to expand VA benefits eligibility, bring more consistency to adjudications of benefits eligibility, and ensure COD determinations consider all pertinent factors."

In the section "II. VA's Decision To Finalize the Proposed Rule With Modifications":
"Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct. The definition for willful and persistent misconduct has been refined for more objective application, and an expanded compelling circumstances exception now applies to both the moral turpitude (MT) and willful and persistent misconduct bars."

Also: "Based upon interagency concerns, VA has decided not to alter the current regulatory bar for MT and does not adopt the language from the proposed rule. This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

This is key - it specifically mentions BCD and OTH discharges as the ones where VA will provide "a case-by-case, more holistic analysis" for benefit eligibility.

Let me check if there's more specific information about which discharges get expanded eligibility.

In the section "Removal of Homosexual Acts Bar":
"VA agrees that the homosexual acts bar is outdated and unnecessary and is entirely removing this regulatory bar. VA is also not adopting the sexual acts bar from the proposed rule, as this misconduct will be sufficiently excluded by either the statutory bars or the remaining regulatory bars."

But this seems to be about removing a specific bar, not directly about which discharge characterizations get expanded eligibility.

In the section "COD Process/Eligibility":
"VA thanks the commenter for the comment, but that statistic is inaccurate. Between October 1, 2019, and September 30, 2022, VA deemed SMs with OTH discharges eligible for healthcare or benefits or both more than 75% of the time."

This suggests OTH discharges are a focus.

Also: "VA believes that, through the modifications of this final rule, including the compelling circumstances exception, it will be able to expand VA benefits eligibility for former SMs with OTH discharges."

And: "In any event, VA anticipates that the amendments in this final rule--including refining the willful and persistent misconduct bar and implementing the compelling circumstances exception for moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct--will increase the number of former SMs eligible for benefits."

Let me look for more specific information about which discharge types are affected.

In the section "Willful and Persistent Misconduct":
"VA clarifies that, even though it uses the term ``minor'' to distinguish one type of misconduct from another, this regulatory bar applies only to former SMs who have not received an Honorable or General (under honorable conditions) discharge. If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Therefore, VA does not bar former SMs simply because they have minor offenses in their record. And even for SMs with a BCD or OTH discharge, VA will not bar benefits for sporadic, minor misconduct, given the definition of ``persistent'' in this final rule."

This suggests the rule changes primarily affect those with BCD or OTH discharges, since those with Honorable or General discharges don't even go through COD determination.

Also: "Accordingly, as a practical matter, VA commits that the only former SMs who will be barred under the willful and persistent misconduct standard of this final rule are those that committed willful, frequent misconduct, which according to documentation in their military discharge records led to their discharge, outweighed the merit of their service, and was not mitigated by any relevant factors."

Let me check the actual regulatory text in Document 3 to see the specific changes.

In the amended Sec. 3.12(d):
"(d) Regulatory bars to benefits. Benefits are not payable where the former service member was discharged or released under one of the conditions listed in paragraph (d)(1) or (2) of this section.
(1) Compelling circumstances exception is not applicable for:
(i) Discharge in lieu of trial. Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial.
(ii) Mutiny or espionage. Mutiny or spying.
(2) Compelling circumstances exception is applicable for:
(i) An offense involving moral turpitude. This paragraph (d)(2)(i) includes, generally, conviction of a felony.
(ii) Willful and persistent misconduct. For purposes of this section, instances of minor misconduct occurring within two years of each other are persistent; an instance of minor misconduct occurring within two years of more serious misconduct is persistent; and instances of more serious misconduct occurring within five years of each other are persistent. For purposes of this section, minor misconduct is misconduct for which the maximum sentence imposable pursuant to the Manual for Courts-Martial United States would not include a dishonorable discharge or confinement for longer than one year if tried by general court-martial."

And then there's a new paragraph (e) for "Compelling circumstances exception" that applies to the bars in (d)(2).

Importantly, the compelling circumstances exception now applies to both moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct bars.

Now, who gets these discharges? The document states: "This final rule precludes benefits eligibility for only those SMs who committed misconduct that renders their service effectively dishonorable."

And: "Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d). That said, a favorable COD determination under this rule does not result in blanket eligibility for all VA benefits or a change in the Department of Defense's (DoD) discharge characterization. Rather, certain VA benefits have specific eligibility requirements as it pertains to COD. For example, education assistance under the Montgomery GI Bill program or Post-9/11 GI Bill program is available only for periods of service resulting in an ``honorable'' discharge. See 38 U.S.C. 3011(a)(3)(B) and 3311(c)(1). Therefore, former SMs who do not receive an Honorable discharge from DoD are ineligible for the VA Education benefit."

This is important - it says former SMs without Honorable discharge are ineligible for VA Education benefit, but may be eligible for other benefits.

Let me find where it specifically says which discharge characterizations get expanded eligibility.

In the section "Support Expanding Benefits Eligibility":
"VA thanks the commenters for these comments. As noted above, VA recognizes the challenging nature of this subject and included it in the RFI for this very reason. Ultimately, after considering the comments for and against further limitation or removal of the regulatory bars to benefits, VA has determined that the provisions of this final rule strike a balance that will better ensure consistency in VA character of discharge determinations while also respecting the Military Departments' interest in disincentivizing significant misconduct prejudicial to good order and discipline. VA recognizes that the Military Departments use the prospect of VA benefits bars as one tool to enforce good order and discipline, and, for that reason, VA has decided not to remove all the regulatory bars, but to remove one and modify one. In that way, the changes in this final rule expand VA benefits to more SMs than ever before, but still align with the necessary incentives for military discipline."

And: "One commenter asked VA to ``[p]lease revise the rules to allow all who have served our country to receive VA Benefits and Healthcare but have been denied based on their character of discharge. Cold War Veterans, and particularly those who served during Vietnam and post-Vietnam were hit hard with many poor leaders. Many [v]eterans suffered significantly from mental health issues during a time in which mental health programs were not readily available, and to those who took advantage where they were available, were given bad paper.'' VA thanks the commenter for their comment. Instances of injustice or inequity in the military about discharges should be addressed to the Boards for Correction of Military Records and/or the Discharge Review Board. That said, the compelling circumstances exception is designed to consider factors like mental impairment and overseas-related hardship, and to consider whether (notwithstanding misconduct) the service was honest, faithful, and meritorious."

The term "bad paper" is often used to refer to OTH and BCD discharges.

Let me check the statutory bars section to understand which discharges are statutorily barred vs. regulatory barred.

In section I. COD Regulatory History:
"Currently, there are six ``statutory bars'' to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12. In addition, currently, there are five ``regulatory bars'' to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12, which states that discharges based on the five listed offenses are ``considered to have been issued under dishonorable conditions.'' The last update to Sec. 3.12(d) occurred in 1980, more than 40 years ago. The 1980 update provided examples of aggravated homosexual acts. 45 FR 2318 (Jan. 11, 1980)."

And: "On July 10, 2020, VA published at 85 FR 41471 its proposal to amend its regulation governing COD determinations. Specifically, VA proposed to modify the regulatory standards for discharges considered ``dishonorable'' for VA benefit eligibility purposes, such as discharges due to ``willful and persistent misconduct,'' and ``homosexual acts involving aggravating circumstances or other factors affecting the performance of duty.'' VA also proposed to extend a ``compelling circumstances'' exception to certain regulatory bars to benefits to ensure consideration of all pertinent factors."

So the regulatory bars are what VA uses to determine if a discharge (that isn't statutorily barred) should still be considered "dishonorable" for VA benefits purposes.

The statutory bars (in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a)) are:
"(1) discharged or dismissed by GCM; (2) discharged for being a conscientious objector who refused to perform military duties, wear the uniform or comply with lawful orders of competent military authorities; (3) a deserter; or (4) as an officer who resigned for the good of the service."

These are absolute bars - no exceptions.

The regulatory bars (in 38 CFR 3.12(d)) are VA's additional bars for discharges that aren't statutorily barred but VA considers "dishonorable."

The 2024 rule reduces the regulatory bars from 5 to 4 (removing the homosexual acts bar), refines the willful and persistent misconduct bar, and adds a compelling circumstances exception for moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct.

Now, which discharge characterizations correspond to these regulatory bars?

Typically:
- Dishonorable discharge and dismissal are statutory bars (from GCM)
- Bad Conduct Discharge (BCD) can be from special or general court-martial
- Other Than Honorable (OTH) is administrative
- General (under honorable conditions) is administrative
- Honorable is administrative

From the document: "Former SMs who do not receive an Honorable discharge from DoD are ineligible for the VA Education benefit." But they may be eligible for other benefits.

And: "VA has decided not to alter the current regulatory bar for MT and does not adopt the language from the proposed rule. This will allow the military to retain a deterrent to misconduct that promotes good order and discipline, while also allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

This is the clearest statement: the expanded eligibility (through the compelling circumstances exception) applies specifically to those with BCD or OTH discharges.

Let me confirm with another part of the document.

In the section "Willful and Persistent Misconduct":
"VA clarifies that, even though it uses the term ``minor'' to distinguish one type of misconduct from another, this regulatory bar applies only to former SMs who have not received an Honorable or General (under honorable conditions) discharge. If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Therefore, VA does not bar former SMs simply because they have minor offenses in their record. And even for SMs with a BCD or OTH discharge, VA will not bar benefits for sporadic, minor misconduct, given the definition of ``persistent'' in this final rule."

This confirms that COD determinations (where the regulatory bars apply) are only for those without Honorable or General discharges - so for those with OTH, BCD, Dishonorable, or Dismissal.

But Dishonorable and Dismissal are statutory bars (from GCM), so they're absolutely barred regardless of regulatory bars.

So the regulatory bars primarily affect those with OTH and BCD discharges.

And the 2024 rule expands eligibility specifically for those with OTH and BCD discharges by:
1. Removing the homosexual acts regulatory bar
2. Refining the willful and persistent misconduct bar to be more objective
3. Adding a compelling circumstances exception for moral turpitude and willful and persistent misconduct bars

The document states: "allowing VA to provide a case-by-case, more holistic analysis of whether a former SM who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge nevertheless warrants ``veteran'' status and VA benefits eligibility."

- Uncharacterized discharges: Document 3 mentions "uncharacterized separations" in section IV: "Per 38 CFR 3.12(k) (redesignated in this rule to Sec. 3.12(l)), there are three types of uncharacterized separations: (1) entry level separation; (2) void enlistment or induction; and (3) dropped from the rolls. So General discharge holders don't go through COD determination at all - they're automatically eligible (except for specific benefits like education that require Honorable discharge).

Let me look for specific mentions of which discharge types get expanded eligibility.

From the document:
"Moreover, while relaxing the bars to eligibility, this final rule does not extend VA benefits eligibility to all former SMs. Former SMs who do not meet the criteria for benefits eligibility may remain entitled to certain critical benefits to address the harms caused by their military service such as mental health and substance use care, emergent suicide care, and medical care in emergency situations, as discussed below."

And:
"VA thanks the commenter for their comment. VA has refined the willful and persistent misconduct bar, as well as implemented a compelling circumstances exception, to distinguish between serious misconduct worthy of a ``dishonorable'' determination and misconduct that is mitigated by the circumstances or outweighed by otherwise meritorious service. The aim is to provide benefits in the latter situation, but not the former."

Let me check the actual regulatory text changes in the document.

At the end of Document 3, it shows the amended regulation:

"(d) Regulatory bars to benefits. Benefits are not payable where the former service member was discharged or released under one of the conditions listed in paragraph (d)(1) or (2) of this section.
(1) Compelling circumstances exception is not applicable for:
(i) Discharge in lieu of trial. Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial.
(ii) Mutiny or espionage. Mutiny or spying.
(2) Compelling circumstances exception is applicable for:
(i) An offense involving moral turpitude. This paragraph (d)(2)(i) includes, generally, conviction of a felony.
(ii) Willful and persistent misconduct. For purposes of this section, instances of minor misconduct occurring within two years of each other are persistent; an instance of minor misconduct occurring within two years of more serious misconduct is persistent; and instances of more serious misconduct occurring within five years of each other are persistent. For purposes of this section, minor misconduct is misconduct for which the maximum sentence imposable pursuant to the Manual for Courts-Martial United States would not include a dishonorable discharge or confinement for longer than one year if tried by general court-martial."

And there's a new paragraph (e) for "Compelling circumstances exception."

Now, let's see which discharge characterizations this affects.

The regulatory bars apply to people who received less than honorable discharges. The statutory bars (which weren't changed) apply to specific situations like conscientious objectors, deserters, etc.

The key is that the regulatory bars are what prevent people with less than honorable discharges from getting benefits. By modifying the regulatory bars (removing one, refining another, adding compelling circumstances exception to two), VA is expanding eligibility for people with less than honorable discharges who would have been barred under the old regulatory bars.

But which specific discharge types?

The document states: "A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

But then it says: "If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a)."

Wait, this is important. Let me check 38 CFR 3.12(a) in the amended regulation.

From the amended regulation text at the end of Document 3:
"(a) General rule. If the former service member did not die in service, then pension, compensation, or dependency and indemnity compensation is payable for claims based on a period of service that was terminated by discharge or release under conditions other than dishonorable. (38 U.S.C. 101(2)) A discharge under honorable conditions is binding on the Department of Veterans Affairs as to character of discharge."

And in the commentary:
"VA clarifies that, even though it uses the term ``minor'' to distinguish one type of misconduct from another, this regulatory bar applies only to former SMs who have not received an Honorable or General (under honorable conditions) discharge. If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Therefore, VA does not bar former SMs simply because they have minor offenses in their record."

So it seems that "General (under honorable conditions)" discharge is NOT subject to COD determination at all - it's considered "under honorable conditions" per the statute.

Let me confirm this.

38 U.S.C. 101(2) defines veteran as someone "discharged or released therefrom under conditions other than dishonorable."

And 38 CFR 3.12(a) says: "A discharge under honorable conditions is binding on the Department of Veterans Affairs as to character of discharge."

The document also states: "If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant."

So "General (under honorable conditions)" is considered "under honorable conditions" and doesn't go through COD determination.

That means the COD determination (and thus the regulatory bars) only applies to:
- Other than honorable (OTH)
- Uncharacterized (but only certain types - see below)
- Bad-conduct discharge (BCD)
- Dishonorable discharge
- Dismissal

But dishonorable discharge and dismissal are statutory bars, not regulatory bars.

Let me check the statutory bars.

From the document: "Currently, there are six ``statutory bars'' to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12."

And in the amended regulation, paragraph (c) lists statutory bars including:
"(2) Discharged or dismissed by reason of the sentence of a general court-martial;"

Which would cover dishonorable discharge and dismissal (since dismissal is for officers, dishonorable for enlisted, both from GCM).

So statutory bars (unchanged by this rule) cover:
- Dishonorable discharge
- Dismissal
- Plus others like conscientious objector, deserter, etc.

The regulatory bars (which WERE changed by this rule) apply to other less than honorable discharges that aren't covered by statutory bars.

From the document: "In addition, currently, there are five ``regulatory bars'' to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12, which states that discharges based on the five listed offenses are ``considered to have been issued under dishonorable conditions.''"

And the final rule reduces this to four regulatory bars.

So the regulatory bars apply to discharges that are less than honorable but not covered by statutory bars - which would be:
- Other than honorable (OTH)
- Bad-conduct discharge (BCD) [though BCD can come from special court-martial, not just GCM]
- Uncharacterized (but only "dropped from the rolls" type, not entry level separation)

Let me check the uncharacterized discharges.

From the document: "Per 38 CFR 3.12(k) (redesignated in this rule to Sec. 3.12(l)), there are three types of uncharacterized separations: (1) entry level separation; (2) void enlistment or induction; and (3) dropped from the rolls. An entry level separation is considered under conditions other than dishonorable; accordingly, this rulemaking does not apply to this type of uncharacterized separation. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Void enlistments are reviewed under the factors listed in 38 CFR 3.14, and thus are also not impacted by this rulemaking. However, when a former SM was dropped from the rolls, the facts and circumstances surrounding the separation must be reviewed to determine whether the separation was under conditions other than dishonorable. These determinations are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking for the reasons discussed above."

So "dropped from the rolls" uncharacterized separations are treated like OTH for COD purposes.

And BCD - the document mentions "SMs with BCD and OTH discharges" multiple times as the ones affected by the regulatory bars.

For example: "VA believes this final rule does so by eliminating one of the regulatory bars, refining another, and applying a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned ``veteran'' status."

Also: "After extensive interagency discussion, VA was advised that Commanders within the Military Departments use the prospect of VA benefits bars as one tool to enforce good order and discipline, and that the Military Departments were concerned that any expansion of VA benefits to former SMs who committed serious misconduct would have the effect of removing disincentives to misconduct. Thus, VA is retaining four of the regulatory bars, with modifications. Those modifications will help distinguish those who committed serious misconduct that renders their service dishonorable from those whose misconduct comes with a mitigating circumstance or is outweighed by otherwise meritorious service. This strikes an appropriate balance: it expands VA benefits eligibility, but also avoids sending a message that misconduct has no repercussions. It aligns with the necessary Military Department incentives for military discipline, while also guaranteeing a more holistic and equitable COD review for former SMs."

And: "VA has determined (after several rounds of public input) that the current regulatory approach to SMs with BCD and OTH discharges needs a restructuring to strike the appropriate balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them, while also limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct."

So the rule specifically expands eligibility for those with:
- Other than honorable (OTH) discharges
- Bad-conduct discharges (BCD)
- "Dropped from the rolls" uncharacterized separations (treated as OTH)

But not for:
- General (under honorable conditions) - not subject to COD at all
- Dishonorable discharge - statutory bar, unchanged
- Dismissal - statutory bar, unchanged
- Entry level separation - considered honorable
- Void enlistment - separate process

Now, the question asks: "For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility?"

From earlier, DARB counts these as less than honorable:
1. General (under honorable conditions)

But VA's rule expands eligibility for:
- OTH
- BCD
- "Dropped from the rolls" uncharacterized (but not entry level or void enlistment)

And does NOT expand for:
- General (under honorable conditions) - not subject to COD
- Dishonorable discharge - statutory bar
- Dismissal - statutory bar
- Entry level separation - considered honorable
- Void enlistment - separate process

The question specifically asks about separations that BOTH:

So from the DARB's list of 6, which ones get expanded eligibility under VA's rule?

- General (under honorable conditions): DARB counts as less than honorable, but VA doesn't even do COD for these - they're automatically eligible (for most benefits). - Other than honorable (OTH): DARB counts as less than honorable, AND VA's rule expands eligibility (via compelling circumstances exception for MT and willful/persistent misconduct bars)

The question is tricky because DARB's definition of "less than honorable" includes General (under honorable conditions), but VA doesn't consider that as requiring COD determination.

Let me check DARB's definition again.

From Document 5, Sec. 73.2:
"Characterization of less than honorable. A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

And footnote 42: "A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

But in VA's system, "general under honorable conditions" is NOT "less than honorable" for COD purposes - it's considered "under honorable conditions" per 38 U.S.C. 101(2).

There's a discrepancy in terminology.

The statute (38 U.S.C. 101(2)) says veteran status requires discharge "under conditions other than dishonorable."

VA regulations (38 CFR 3.12(a)) say: "A discharge under honorable conditions is binding on the Department of Veterans Affairs as to character of discharge."

So for VA, "honorable conditions" includes both Honorable and General (under honorable conditions) discharges.

This is a key point.

The question asks: "For which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board counts as less than honorable does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility?"

VA's rule expands eligibility for some discharges that VA considers subject to COD determination (i.e., not honorable conditions).

VA considers subject to COD: OTH, BCD, dropped-from-rolls uncharacterized

VA does NOT consider subject to COD: Honorable, General (under hon cond), entry level separation, void enlistment

VA considers statutory bars (no COD, automatically barred): dishonorable, dismissal, plus others

So for DARB's "less than honorable" list:

1. General (under hon cond): DARB calls it less than honorable, but VA considers it "honorable conditions" so no COD needed, already eligible. Rule doesn't expand eligibility (it was already there).

2. OTH: DARB calls less than honorable, VA does COD, rule expands eligibility via compelling circumstances.

3. Uncharacterized: DARB calls less than honorable. But only "dropped from rolls" type gets COD and expanded eligibility. Entry level and void enlistment don't. Since DARB just says "uncharacterized" generally, but VA treats subtypes differently.

4. BCD: DARB calls less than honorable, VA does COD (for BCD from special CM), rule expands eligibility.

5. Dishonorable: DARB calls less than honorable, but statutory bar, rule doesn't change.

6. Dismissal: DARB calls less than honorable, but statutory bar (for officers), rule doesn't change.

Full answer
-----------

To determine which separations counted as "less than honorable" by DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) have expanded benefit eligibility under VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule, I need to compare the DARB's definition of "less than honorable" with the specific discharges affected by VA's 2024 regulatory changes.

First, the DARB explicitly defines "Characterization of less than honorable" in 32 CFR 73.2 as including: "a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal" (Document 5).

However, VA's 2024 rule (Document 3, effective June 25, 2024) only modifies regulatory bars to benefits, not statutory bars. As Document 3 explains: "Currently, there are six 'statutory bars' to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12. In addition, currently, there are five 'regulatory bars' to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12, which states that discharges based on the five listed offenses are 'considered to have been issued under dishonorable conditions.'" Dishonorable discharges and dismissals are statutory bars under 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) that VA cannot modify through regulation.

Document 3 repeatedly specifies which discharges benefit from the 2024 changes: "VA believes this final rule does so by eliminating one of the regulatory bars, refining another, and applying a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned 'veteran' status." This "BCD or OTH discharge" formulation appears multiple times in the document.

Regarding uncharacterized separations, Document 3 states: "Per 38 CFR 3.12(k) (redesignated in this rule to Sec. 3.12(l)), there are three types of uncharacterized separations: (1) entry level separation; (2) void enlistment or induction; and (3) dropped from the rolls... However, when a former SM was dropped from the rolls, the facts and circumstances surrounding the separation must be reviewed to determine whether the separation was under conditions other than dishonorable. These determinations are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking for the reasons discussed above."

Meanwhile, Document 3 clarifies that "General under honorable conditions" discharges are not subject to character-of-discharge determinations at all: "If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a)." Thus, the 2024 rule doesn't "expand" eligibility for these discharges since no restriction existed.

Therefore, among the DARB's six "less than honorable" categories, VA's 2024 rule expands benefit eligibility specifically for:

1. Other than honorable (OTH) conditions discharges
2. Bad-conduct discharges (BCD)
3. Uncharacterized separations resulting from being "dropped from the rolls" (which VA adjudicates using the OTH framework)

<answer>Other than honorable (OTH) discharges, bad-conduct discharges (BCD), and uncharacterized separations resulting from being "dropped from the rolls" (which VA adjudicates as OTH discharges)</answer>

Reasoning
---------

Let me analyze this question carefully. The question is asking about which separations that DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) counts as "less than honorable" does VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule expand benefit eligibility for.

First, I need to identify:
1. What separations the DARB counts as "less than honorable"
2. What VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule changed regarding benefit eligibility
3. The intersection of these two - which specific separations counted as "less than honorable" by DARB now have expanded benefit eligibility under VA's 2024 rule

Let me search through the documents for information about the DARB and what it considers "less than honorable".

From Document 5 (Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 230, November 29, 2024), which describes the DoD Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB):

In the definitions section (Sec. 73.2), it states:
"Characterization of less than honorable. A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

So according to the DARB rule, the following are considered "less than honorable":
1. General under honorable conditions
2. Other than honorable conditions (OTH)
3. Uncharacterized
4. Bad-conduct discharge (BCD)
5. Dishonorable discharge
6. Dismissal

Now, let me look for VA's 2024 character-of-discharge rule. Document 3 appears to be about this - it's from Federal Register, Volume 89 Issue 82 (Friday, April 26, 2024) titled "Update and Clarify Regulatory Bars to Benefits Based on Character of Discharge".

From Document 3:
"This final rule is effective June 25, 2024. Applicability date: The provisions of this final rule shall apply to all applications for benefits that are received by VA on or after June 25, 2024, or that are pending before VA, the United States Court of Appeals for Veterans Claims, or the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit (Federal Circuit) on June 25, 2024."

And in the summary:
"After considering public comments, VA has decided to finalize its proposal with some modifications to expand VA benefits eligibility, bring more consistency to adjudications of benefits eligibility, and ensure COD determinations consider all pertinent factors."

Let me find the specific changes to the regulatory bars.

From Document 3:
"Thus, with this final rule, there will be only four regulatory bars: (1) acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by GCM; (2) mutiny or spying; (3) moral turpitude; and (4) willful and persistent misconduct."

Previously, there were five regulatory bars, including one for "homosexual acts". The document states: "VA agrees that the homosexual acts bar is outdated and unnecessary and is entirely removing this regulatory bar. VA is also not adopting the sexual acts bar from the proposed rule, as this misconduct will be sufficiently excluded by either the statutory bars or the remaining regulatory bars."

Also: "The definition for willful and persistent misconduct has been refined for more objective application, and an expanded compelling circumstances exception now applies to both the moral turpitude (MT) and willful and persistent misconduct bars."

And: "Based upon interagency concerns, VA has decided not to alter the current regulatory bar for MT and does not adopt the language from the proposed rule."

Let me find more details about what specific discharges are affected.

From Document 3:
"Overall, under this final rule, more SMs will be eligible for benefits than under the prior 38 CFR 3.12(d). That said, a favorable COD determination under this rule does not result in blanket eligibility for all VA benefits or a change in the Department of Defense's (DoD) discharge characterization. Rather, certain VA benefits have specific eligibility requirements as it pertains to COD. For example, education assistance under the Montgomery GI Bill program or Post-9/11 GI Bill program is available only for periods of service resulting in an ``honorable'' discharge. See 38 U.S.C. 3011(a)(3)(B) and 3311(c)(1). Therefore, former SMs who do not receive an Honorable discharge from DoD are ineligible for the VA Education benefit."

This is important - it says education benefits still require honorable discharge.

But for other benefits, the rule expands eligibility.

Let me look for specific mention of which discharges now have expanded eligibility.

From Document 3:
"Moreover, while relaxing the bars to eligibility, this final rule does not extend VA benefits eligibility to all former SMs. Former SMs who do not meet the criteria for benefits eligibility may remain entitled to certain critical benefits to address the harms caused by their military service such as mental health and substance use care, emergent suicide care, and medical care in emergency situations, as discussed below."

And:
"As noted above, VA aims to strike an appropriate balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them and limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct. VA believes this final rule does so by eliminating one of the regulatory bars, refining another, and applying a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned ``veteran'' status."

Ah, here it specifically mentions "BCD or OTH discharge" as the ones where the rule provides a more holistic assessment.

Let me check if there are other mentions.

From Document 3:
"One commenter stated that ``[c]hanges to VA shouldn't be bureaucratic, they should be legislative. In addition, Veterans should serve honorably throughout their contract otherwise they shouldn't be entitled to VA benefits.'' VA thanks the commenter for their comment. As discussed above, Congress delegated to VA the ability to set criteria for what constitutes ``other than dishonorable'' service for purposes of VA benefits eligibility. This rulemaking is necessary to refine those criteria. VA makes no changes to the regulatory text based on this comment."

And:
"Similarly, a few commenters stated that former SMs with ``Bad Paper,'' OTH or dishonorable discharges should not be eligible for VA benefits, do not deserve any VA assistance and that their eligibility may delay the receipt of care for former SMs with honorable service. VA thanks these commenters for their comments. As noted above, VA aims to strike an appropriate balance between bestowing benefits to those who have earned them and limiting benefits for those whose service involved serious misconduct. VA believes this final rule does so by eliminating one of the regulatory bars, refining another, and applying a compelling circumstances exception to two of the regulatory bars, which provides a more holistic assessment of all appropriate factors in determining whether a former SM, despite a BCD or OTH discharge, has nevertheless earned ``veteran'' status."

Again, specifically mentions "BCD or OTH discharge".

Also: "Another commenter opined that ``[u]nless a discharge is upgraded, every OTH, BC[D], and D[ishonorable] D[ischarge] should be barred from getting any VA benefit."

And VA's response: "VA has refined the willful and persistent misconduct bar, as well as implemented a compelling circumstances exception, to distinguish between serious misconduct worthy of a ``dishonorable'' determination and misconduct that is mitigated by the circumstances or outweighed by otherwise meritorious service. The aim is to provide benefits in the latter situation, but not the former."

So it seems the main expansion is for OTH (Other Than Honorable) and BCD (Bad Conduct Discharge) discharges.

But let me check if "general under honorable conditions" is also affected.

From Document 3:
"If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Therefore, VA does not bar former SMs simply because they have minor offenses in their record."

This suggests that General (under honorable conditions) discharges are not subject to COD (Character of Discharge) determinations at all - they're automatically eligible. So the rule change wouldn't affect them because they weren't previously barred.

Let me confirm this.

From Document 3, in the section about "Willful and Persistent Misconduct":
"VA clarifies that, even though it uses the term ``minor'' to distinguish one type of misconduct from another, this regulatory bar applies only to former SMs who have not received an Honorable or General (under honorable conditions) discharge. If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a)."

So General (under honorable conditions) discharges don't go through COD determination at all - they're automatically considered "under conditions other than dishonorable".

What about "uncharacterized" discharges?

From Document 3, in section IV: "Uncharacterized Discharges and Coast Guard Discharges":
"Per 38 CFR 3.12(k) (redesignated in this rule to Sec. 3.12(l)), there are three types of uncharacterized separations: (1) entry level separation; (2) void enlistment or induction; and (3) dropped from the rolls. An entry level separation is considered under conditions other than dishonorable; accordingly, this rulemaking does not apply to this type of uncharacterized separation. See 38 CFR 3.12(a). Void enlistments are reviewed under the factors listed in 38 CFR 3.14, and thus are also not impacted by this rulemaking.
However, when a former SM was dropped from the rolls, the facts and circumstances surrounding the separation must be reviewed to determine whether the separation was under conditions other than dishonorable. These determinations are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking for the reasons discussed above."

So "dropped from the rolls" (one type of uncharacterized separation) is treated like OTH discharge and would be favorably impacted.

But the other two types of uncharacterized separations (entry level separation and void enlistment) are not impacted.

What about dishonorable discharge and dismissal?

From Document 3: "VA thanks the commenters for their comments and assures the commenters that those who received a Dishonorable discharge from the military are excluded from benefits eligibility."

And: "Another commenter stated that ``[c]hanges to VA shouldn't be bureaucratic, they should be legislative. In addition, Veterans should serve honorably throughout their contract otherwise they shouldn't be entitled to VA benefits.'' VA thanks the commenter for their comment. As discussed above, Congress delegated to VA the ability to set criteria for what constitutes ``other than dishonorable'' service for purposes of VA benefits eligibility. This rulemaking is necessary to refine those criteria. VA makes no changes to the regulatory text based on this comment."

Also: "Similarly, a few commenters stated that former SMs with ``Bad Paper,'' OTH or dishonorable discharges should not be eligible for VA benefits..."

And VA's response indicates they're not extending eligibility to dishonorable discharges.

From the regulatory text in Document 3, Sec. 3.12(d):
"(d) Regulatory bars to benefits. Benefits are not payable where the former service member was discharged or released under one of the conditions listed in paragraph (d)(1) or (2) of this section.
(1) Compelling circumstances exception is not applicable for:
(i) Discharge in lieu of trial. Acceptance of a discharge under other than honorable conditions or its equivalent in lieu of trial by general court-martial.
(ii) Mutiny or espionage. Mutiny or spying.
(2) Compelling circumstances exception is applicable for:
(i) An offense involving moral turpitude. This paragraph (d)(2)(i) includes, generally, conviction of a felony.
(ii) Willful and persistent misconduct. For purposes of this section, instances of minor misconduct occurring within two years of each other are persistent; an instance of minor misconduct occurring within two years of more serious misconduct is persistent; and instances of more serious misconduct occurring within five years of each other are persistent. For purposes of this section, minor misconduct is misconduct for which the maximum sentence imposable pursuant to the Manual for Courts-Martial United States would not include a dishonorable discharge or confinement for longer than one year if tried by general court-martial."

And in the statutory bars (c):
"(c) Statutory bars to benefits. Benefits are not payable where the former service member was discharged or released under one of the following conditions:
...
(6) By reason of a discharge under other than honorable conditions issued as a result of an absence without official leave (AWOL) for a continuous period of at least 180 days (38 U.S.C. 5303(a))."

But dishonorable discharge is a statutory bar under 38 U.S.C. 5303(a), which VA cannot change through regulation.

From Document 3: "Currently, there are six ``statutory bars'' to benefits for former SMs listed in 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) and reiterated in paragraph (c) of 38 CFR 3.12."

And 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) includes: "(1) Dishonorable discharge... (5) Dismissal..."

So dishonorable discharge and dismissal are statutory bars that VA cannot override through regulation.

Let me confirm this.

From Document 3: "In addition, currently, there are five ``regulatory bars'' to benefits listed in paragraph (d) of 38 CFR 3.12, which states that discharges based on the five listed offenses are ``considered to have been issued under dishonorable conditions.''"

So the statutory bars (including dishonorable discharge and dismissal) are set by Congress and VA cannot change them through regulation. The regulatory bars are VA's own interpretation of what constitutes "dishonorable conditions" for other discharge types.

The VA's 2024 rule modified the regulatory bars, not the statutory bars.

So for discharges that are statutory bars (dishonorable discharge, dismissal), VA cannot expand eligibility through regulation.

For discharges that are not statutory bars but were subject to regulatory bars (OTH, BCD, and "dropped from the rolls" uncharacterized separations), VA can modify the regulatory bars.

From the DARB definition, "less than honorable" includes:
1. General under honorable conditions - but these don't go through COD determination at all, so not affected by regulatory bar changes
2. Other than honorable conditions (OTH) - subject to regulatory bars, now with expanded compelling circumstances exception
3. Uncharacterized - but only "dropped from the rolls" type is treated like OTH; entry level and void enlistment are not
4. Bad-conduct discharge (BCD) - subject to regulatory bars, now with expanded compelling circumstances exception
5. Dishonorable discharge - statutory bar, cannot be changed by VA regulation
6. Dismissal - statutory bar, cannot be changed by VA regulation

So the separations that DARB counts as "less than honorable" for which VA's 2024 rule expands benefit eligibility are:
- Other than honorable conditions (OTH)
- Bad-conduct discharge (BCD)
- Uncharacterized separations of the "dropped from the rolls" type (which are treated as OTH)

(1) The Service member's date of discharge or dismissal was on or after December 20, 2019;
(2) Service member received a less than honorable characterization of service at the time of discharge or dismissal;"

So DARB considers all six as "less than honorable" for its review purposes.

But VA's rule only expands eligibility for some of these.

But let me check if "general under honorable conditions" is ever subject to COD determination.

From Document 3: "If a SM has an Honorable or General discharge, VA does not conduct a COD determination and this bar is irrelevant. See 38 CFR 3.12(a)."

And 38 CFR 3.12(a) in the new rule: "(a) General rule. If the former service member did not die in service, then pension, compensation, or dependency and indemnity compensation is payable for claims based on a period of service that was terminated by discharge or release under conditions other than dishonorable. (38 U.S.C. 101(2)) A discharge under honorable conditions is binding on the Department of Veterans Affairs as to character of discharge."

"Discharge under honorable conditions" includes both Honorable and General (under honorable conditions) discharges.

So General discharges are explicitly "under honorable conditions" and thus not subject to COD determination at all.

Therefore, the DARB counts General as "less than honorable" but VA doesn't consider it a bar to benefits - it's automatically eligible. So the 2024 rule doesn't "expand" eligibility for General discharges because there was no restriction to begin with.

The expansion is specifically for discharges that were previously subject to regulatory bars but now have a more favorable analysis.

Full answer
-----------

Separations on DARB's "Less Than Honorable" List for Which VA's 2024 COD Rule Expands Eligibility

DoD's DARB (32 CFR 73.2) defines "characterization of less than honorable" as encompassing: general (under honorable conditions), other than honorable (OTH), uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, and dismissal.

Cross-referencing with VA's 2024 character-of-discharge final rule (38 CFR 3.12), the rule expands benefit eligibility for three of these:

1. Other than honorable (OTH) conditions — The rule removes the homosexual acts regulatory bar, adds a compelling-circumstances exception to the moral-turpitude and willful-and-persistent-misconduct bars, and refines the willful-and-persistent-misconduct definition, all of which increase the number of OTH-discharged service members who can establish veteran status.
2. Bad-conduct discharge (BCD) — BCD holders are not statutorily barred (they receive BCDs from special courts-martial, not general courts-martial) and undergo the same COD review. The same modifications (bar removal, compelling-circumstances exception, refined WPM criteria) expand eligibility for BCD recipients.
3. Uncharacterized (specifically, those "dropped from the rolls") — The rule states these determinations are conducted in the same manner as OTH discharges and "will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking." (Entry-level separations and void enlistments are unaffected.)

Not expanded:

- General (under honorable conditions) — already binding on VA as eligible under 38 CFR 3.12(a); no expansion needed.
- Dishonorable discharge — issued only by general court-martial and statutorily barred under 38 U.S.C. 5303(a) / 38 CFR 3.12(c)(2); the regulatory-bar changes do not alter the statutory bars.
- Dismissal — the officer equivalent of a dishonorable discharge, also from general court-martial and thus statutorily barred.

Full answer
-----------

Separations with Expanded Benefit Eligibility Under VA's 2024 Character-of-Discharge Rule

Based on Document 3 (the April 2024 VA final rule amending 38 CFR 3.12), the rule expands benefit eligibility for the following less-than-honorable separations as characterized by DoD:

1. Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharges — The rule removes the homosexual acts regulatory bar entirely, refines the willful and persistent misconduct bar with objective time-frame criteria, and adds a compelling circumstances exception to the moral turpitude and willful/persistent misconduct bars, making it easier for OTH-discharged members to be found eligible.
2. Bad Conduct Discharges (BCD) — Same modifications apply. The preamble states the rule helps "distinguish those who committed serious misconduct that renders their service dishonorable from those whose misconduct comes with a mitigating circumstance or is outweighed by otherwise meritorious service," specifically referencing "former SMs who received a Bad-Conduct Discharge (BCD) or Other Than Honorable (OTH) discharge."
3. Uncharacterized separations (dropped from the rolls) — Section IV of the rule states these determinations "are conducted in the same manner as if such former SM received an OTH discharge. Accordingly, these former SMs will be favorably impacted by this rulemaking."

Not affected:

- Dishonorable discharges — remain statutorily barred (38 U.S.C. 5303(a)); no regulatory change helps these.
- Entry-level separations and void enlistments (uncharacterized) — not impacted, as the former is already "under conditions other than dishonorable" and the latter is reviewed under a different framework (38 CFR 3.14).

Full answer
-----------

Answer

Based on the provided documents, the DoD's Discharge Appeal Review Board (DARB) defines "less than honorable" as including (Document 4, 32 CFR 73.2):

1. General under honorable conditions
2. Other than honorable conditions
3. Uncharacterized (entry-level separation)
4. Bad-conduct discharge
5. Dishonorable discharge
6. Dismissal

Regarding VA benefit eligibility tied to character of discharge, Document 4 (the 2024 DARB interim final rule) states that holders of other than honorable conditions (OTH) discharges "are not automatically eligible for VA disability compensation, access to VA home loans, or medical care at VA facilities," but "may apply to the VA for consideration of these benefits and the VA makes this eligibility determination on a fact-specific basis" (footnote 47). This pathway—from no automatic eligibility to case-by-case consideration—constitutes the expanded benefit eligibility described in the documents.

The documents also note that GI Bill educational benefits require an honorable characterization, with "general (under honorable conditions)" explicitly not qualifying; no other less-than-honorable category is described as having expanded VA benefit eligibility.

Complete list

Other than honorable conditions (OTH) — for VA disability compensation, VA home loans, and medical care at VA facilities.

---------

"Characterization of less than honorable. A characterization that is less than honorable includes a general under honorable conditions, other than honorable conditions, uncharacterized, bad-conduct discharge, dishonorable discharge, or a dismissal."

Also from the footnote: "A less than honorable discharge characterization includes dismissals, a general, other than honorable, uncharacterized, bad-conduct, and dishonorable discharges."

So the DARB's "less than honorable" list is:
1. General under honorable conditions
2. Other than honorable conditions
3. Uncharacterized (entry-level separation)
4. Bad-conduct discharge
5. Dishonorable discharge
6. Dismissal
